\gamma>\|K\|_{L_{1}}M_{\infty}^{\prime}
, around the origin into a centered and scaled logistic function; specifically, this
\Psi
is given by
\Psi(t)=t
for
|t|\leq\gamma
and
\Psi(t)=\text{sgn}(t)\gamma-1/2+1/[1+\exp\{-4(t-\text{sgn}(t)\gamma)\}]
for
|t|>\gamma
.

A.3
Miscellaneous results

Lemma A.1
.

Under Assumption
A.3
, for generic functions
g=g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}):\mathbb{R}^{{\overline{r}}+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
m=m({\bm{f}},x_{j}):\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
c^{\textup{s}}
(that does not depend on
g
or
m
) such that

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\int_{\Omega_{h}}g_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\leq\frac{(c^{\textup{s}})^{2}}{h^{2}}\int g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P},\\
\textstyle\int_{\Omega_{h}}\{g_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\leq\frac{(c^{\textup{s}})^{2}}{h^{2}}\int\{g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}.\end{gathered}

(38)

Proof.

We only prove the first half of (
38
); the second half will follow by a similar argument. We have, starting from (
10
) and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\Omega_{h}}g_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}=\textstyle\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left\{\frac{1}{h}\int_{-1}^{1}g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-ah)\dot{K}(a){\rm d}a\right\}^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left\{\int_{-1}^{1}g^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-ah){\rm d}a\right\}\left\{\int_{-1}^{1}\dot{K}(a)^{2}{\rm d}a\right\}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\lesssim\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left\{\int_{-1}^{1}g^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-ah){\rm d}a\right\}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\left\{\int_{\Omega_{h}}g^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-ah)\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right\}{\rm d}a\leq\frac{2}{h^{2}}\|g\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.\qed

Lemma A.2

(Numerical approximation for candidate Riesz representer derivative in Section
2.4
)
.

Let
N
be a large but fixed integer (we used
N=50
throughout our numerical studies) and consider the grid points
a_{1},\dots,a_{2N}
where
a_{l}=-1-\frac{1}{2N}+\frac{l}{N}
(these grid points are the centers of the
2N
equally-sized intervals that form a partition of the interval
[-1,1]
). Then,
\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
in Section
2.4
can be numerically approximated via a middle Riemann sum over the said grid points as

\displaystyle\textstyle\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\approx\frac{1}{Nh}\sum_{l=1}^{2N}\dot{K}\left(a_{l}\right)\alpha\left(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}-a_{l}h\right).

Then, the sample average of the
\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
’s in
\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}
in (
12
) can be approximated as

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\approx\frac{1}{nNh}\sum_{l=1}^{2N}\dot{K}\left(a_{l}\right)\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{h}}\alpha\left(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}-a_{l}h\right)\right\}.

Proof.

We only prove the first equation display, because the second equation display is straightforward given the first. Starting from the right-most term in (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{-1}^{1}\alpha(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}-ah)\dot{K}(a){\rm d}a\approx\frac{1}{Nh}\sum_{l=1}^{2N}\dot{K}\left(a_{l}\right)\alpha\left(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}-a_{l}h\right),

where the approximation follows by replacing the integral with the middle Riemann sum over the grid points in the lemma statement, where each grid point carries a weight
2/(2N)=1/N
.
∎

Lemma A.3

(Existence and analytical form of the Riesz representer in (
25
))
.

Under Assumptions
A.3
and
A.5
, the Riesz representer
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
that satisfies (
25
) exists. Furthermore, if
\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}
admits a joint density
p=p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
, then
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
takes the form

\displaystyle\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})=\dfrac{\int_{\mathcal{B}_{h}}\dot{K}_{h}(z-x_{j})e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z)}p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z}{p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}.

(39)

Finally, if
p
is bounded and is differentiable in
x_{j}
with the derivative being
p_{j}
, then under
H_{0}
and in the limit
h\rightarrow 0
, (
39
) simplifies to
-p_{j}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})/p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
.

Proof.

We first verify that
\frac{1}{t}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[v]
is a bounded linear functional. For any fixed
t
and
h
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[v]=\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0}+\tau v)}{\partial\tau}\Big|_{\tau=0}\textstyle=\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\int_{\Omega_{h}}e^{t\,\Psi\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]\Big|_{\tau=0}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left[e^{t\,\Psi\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}\dot{\Psi}\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}\right]\Big|_{\tau=0}tv_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle=t\int_{\Omega_{h}}e^{t\,\Psi\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}\dot{\Psi}\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}v_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

Now, we note that equivalent to (
10
) one can also write

\displaystyle\textstyle g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})=\int_{-1}^{1}m_{0,j}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-ah)K(a){\rm d}a.

Thus, clearly

|g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})|\leq\|K\|_{L_{1}}\|m_{0,j}\|_{L_{\infty}}
whenever
x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}
. Analogously,
|m_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{f}},x_{j})|\leq\|K\|_{L_{1}}\|m_{0,j}\|_{L_{\infty}}
whenever
x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}
as well. Thus, by Assumptions
A.1
and
A.5
,
\Psi\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}=g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
and
\dot{\Psi}\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}=1
; hence, we conclude that

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[v]=t\int_{\Omega_{h}}e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}v_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

(40)

Thus, the functional
\frac{1}{t}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[v]
is clearly linear in its input and, by the boundedness of
g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}
just shown and Lemma
A.1
, is also bounded.
Therefore, the Riesz representer that satisfies (
25
) exists
(
Conway,, 1990
, Theorem 3.4, Chapter 1)
. As a side remark, the modified functional that, when acting on any
v
, always returns
\int_{\Omega_{h}}v_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}
(the last term in (
25
)) is also a bounded linear functional. Hence, this modified functional admits its own Riesz representer. It’s also easy to show that the latter Riesz representer minimizes the population loss
R_{n}^{\textup{null}}\equiv\int\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha){\rm d}\mathbb{P}
. Thus the latter Riesz representer is precisely
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
which we have defined in Section
3.2.1
as the population limit of
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
.

Next, starting from the first two steps of (
25
), with the assumed density
p
,

\displaystyle\textstyle t\langle v,\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle=\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[v]=t\int_{[-b,b]^{\overline{r}}}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{h}}\{\int_{[-b,b]}v(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z)\dot{K}_{h}(x_{j}-z){\rm d}z\}e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}x_{j}{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{f}}

\displaystyle\textstyle=t\int_{[-b,b]^{{\overline{r}}+1}}v(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z)\{\int_{\mathcal{B}_{h}}\dot{K}_{h}(x_{j}-z)e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}x_{j}\}{\rm d}z{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{f}}

\displaystyle=t\int_{[-b,b]^{{\overline{r}}+1}}v(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z)\left\{\dfrac{\int_{\mathcal{B}_{h}}\dot{K}_{h}(x_{j}-z)e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}x_{j}}{p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z)}\right\}p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{f}}.

After interchanging the dummy integration variables
z
and
x_{j}
in the last line above, the term in the curly bracket then gives the form of
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
in (
39
). Finally,

\displaystyle\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})=\frac{\int_{-b+h}^{b-h}\dot{K}_{h}\left(z-x_{j}\right)e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z)}p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z}{p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{under}\penalty\ H_{0}}}{{=}}}\frac{\int_{-b+h}^{b-h}\dot{K}_{h}\left(z-x_{j}\right)p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z}{p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}.

For
x_{j}\in[-b+2h,b-2h]
, the numerator can be treated as

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{-b+h}^{b-h}\dot{K}_{h}\left(z-x_{j}\right)p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z=-\int_{x_{j}-h}^{x_{j}+h}\dot{K}_{h}\left(x_{j}-z\right)p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\int_{x_{j}-h}^{x_{j}+h}K_{h}\left(x_{j}-z\right)p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},z){\rm d}z

\displaystyle\textstyle=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\int_{x_{j}-h}^{x_{j}+h}K_{h}\left(z\right)p(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-z){\rm d}z=-\int_{x_{j}-h}^{x_{j}+h}K_{h}\left(z\right)p_{j}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}-z){\rm d}z\xrightarrow{h\rightarrow 0}-p_{j}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}),

where we have applied twice differentiation under the integral sign
(
Protter and Morrey, Jr.,, 2012
, Theorem 3, Chapter 8)
, followed by the usual approximation to the identity theory
(
Stein and Shakarchi,, 2009
, Theorem 2.1)
. This completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

A.4
Additional remarks

Remark A.1

(Specification test in factor models)
.

As discussed in Section
2.1
, our hypotheses (
3
) and (
4
) are equivalent to determining whether the
j
-th idiosyncratic term
u_{j}
of
{\bm{u}}
(defined in (
1
)) contributes to the regression function at the population level. In the framework of
Fan and Liao, (2022)
, this test is further similar to testing their Factor Augmented Regression (FAR) model against their Factor Augmented Sparse Throughput (FAST) model for high-dimensional regression. To establish the aforementioned equivalence, let’s define the regression function

\displaystyle m_{0}^{\dagger}({\bm{F}},u_{j})=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},u_{j}]

and the associated partial derivative with respect to the last argument:

\textstyle m_{0,j}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},v_{j})=\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{j}}m_{0}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},v_{j}).

(41)

Then, similar to our hypotheses (
3
) and (
4
), the new hypotheses for
u_{j}
can be formulated as

\displaystyle H_{0}^{\dagger}:m_{0,j}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},v_{j})=0\penalty\ \mbox{for all}\penalty\ {\bm{f}},v_{j},\quad\mbox{against}\quad H_{A}^{\dagger}:m_{0,j}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},v_{j})\neq 0\penalty\ \mbox{for some}\penalty\ {\bm{f}},v_{j}.

(42)

Let
\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}
denote the
j
-th row of the loading matrix
\mathbf{B}
, so
X_{j}=\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{F}}+u_{j}
. Clearly,
{\bm{F}},X_{j}
and
{\bm{F}},u_{j}
generate the same
\sigma
-algebra, and hence the functions
m_{0}
and
m_{0}^{\dagger}
as conditional expectations are essentially a re-writing of each other with different arguments. Specifically,

\displaystyle m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})=m_{0}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},x_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}})=m_{0}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},v_{j}).

Thus, the partial derivatives
m_{0,j}
and
m_{0,j}^{\dagger}
are linked as follows: at
x_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}}=v_{j}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle m_{0,j}({\bm{f}},x_{j})=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}m_{0}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},x_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}})=m_{0,j}^{\dagger}({\bm{f}},v_{j}).

Therefore, at least at the population level, our conditional screening hypotheses (
3
) and (
4
) for
m_{0,j}
are equivalent to the similar ones in (
42
) for
m_{0,j}^{\dagger}
.

Remark A.2

(Relationship between (
2
) and the full model)
.

Note that our reduced regression model (
2
) obviously does not preclude the “full” regression model
Y=m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})+\epsilon^{*}
where
m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}}]
is the full conditional expectation. It is apparent that our reduced model and the full model are linked as

\displaystyle\epsilon=\epsilon^{*}+\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}}]-\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\epsilon^{*}+m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j}).

Moreover, by the definition of the conditional expectation,
\epsilon
is centered and uncorrelated with any function of
{\bm{F}}
and
X_{j}
.

Remark A.3

(Boundary region)
.

Technically, we need to exclude the boundary region, which under Assumption
3.1
is the union
[-b,-b+h]\cup[b-h,b]
, from our smoothing operation, because smoothing at a
x_{j}
in this region will extend beyond the support of
X_{j}
which is problematic. Thus the sets
\mathcal{B}_{h}
,
\mathcal{I}_{h}
,
\Omega_{h}
relevant to the interior regions in different contexts were introduced. For instance, the quantity
\int_{\Omega_{h}}g{\rm d}\mathbb{P}
signifies the integral of the function
g
in the interior region only.

Remark A.4

(Cross validation for bandwidth selection)
.

In this remark we provide some brief guideline on how to choose an optimal bandwidth
h
through cross validation. Suppose that we split the data evenly into
K
folds (for instance,
K=5
) indexed by
k=1,\dots,K
, and denote the collection of sample indices
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
within the
k
-th fold by
\mathcal{I}_{k}
. We let
\widehat{g}_{n}^{(-k)}=\widehat{g}_{n}^{(-k)}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
be the regression function estimator similar to that in (
7
), except that here we exclude the samples in the
k
-th fold; then, let
\widehat{g}_{n,j,h}^{(-k),\textup{s}}=\widehat{g}_{n,j,h}^{(-k),\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
be the corresponding estimator for the partial derivative (with respect to
x_{j}
) that is smoothed at bandwidth
h
. Next, let
\widehat{g}_{n}^{(k)}
be the regression function estimator using only the sample in the
k
-th fold, and let
\widehat{g}_{n,j}^{(k)}
be the corresponding unsmoothed estimator for the partial derivative (with respect to
x_{j}
). Then, we define the cross validation error at
h
as
\textup{CV}(h)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}[\widehat{g}_{n,j,h}^{(-k),\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-\widehat{g}_{n,j}^{(k)}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})]^{2}
. Finally, we could look for the value of
h
that minimizes
\textup{CV}(h)
as the optimal
h
.

A.5
Further motivation on the smoothing operation

In this section, we first provide detailed descriptions for our observation in Section
2.3
on the sawtooth function
\zeta_{L}
which demonstrates the intricacy of derivative estimation with deep neural networks, and then further motivate our smoothing operation. By Proposition 2 in
Yarotsky, (2017)
, the triangular function
\zeta:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
defined as

\displaystyle\zeta(t)=\begin{cases}2t,&0\leq t<1/2,\\
2(1-t),&1/2\leq t\leq 1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

is given in terms of the ReLU function
\sigma
by
\zeta(t)=2\sigma(t)-4\sigma(t-1/2)+2\sigma(t-1)
.
Next, let
\zeta_{L}(\cdot)=\frac{2}{2^{L}}\zeta_{L}\circ\zeta_{L}\circ\cdots\circ\zeta_{L}
denote the
L
-fold iteration of
\zeta
that is further scaled by an overall factor
\frac{2}{2^{L}}
. The function
\zeta_{L}
, described in Lemma 2.4 in
Telgarsky, (2015)
, is often referred to as the “sawtooth” function. (To be precise, in that reference,
\zeta_{L}
is defined without the scaling factor
\frac{2}{2^{L}}
.) The function
\zeta_{L}
is visually represented in Figure
A.1
that in particular illustrates the increasingly “oscillatory” behavior of
\zeta_{L}
for increasing values of
L
. By Proposition 2 in
Yarotsky, (2017)
, it’s feasible to implement
\zeta_{L}
with a neural network of no more than
L
hidden layers and three nodes per layer. (See Fig. 2(c) in
Yarotsky, (2017)
for a related construction.)

Figure A.1
:
Iterated triangular functions
\zeta_{L}
in Section
A.5
for
L=1,3,5
. As
L
increases, the
L_{2}
norm of the function
\zeta_{L}
itself decays exponentially fast to zero, while the
L_{2}
norm of the derivative function
\dot{\zeta}_{L}
remains a constant.

The function
\zeta_{L}
essentially consists of
2^{L}
piecewise linear segments with each segment having a slope of constant magnitude
2
. Consequently, the
L_{2}
norm of
\zeta_{L}
over the interval
[0,1]
with respect to the Lebesgue measure is
\{2^{L}\int_{0}^{1/(2^{L})}(2t)^{2}{\rm d}t\}^{1/2}\sim 1/2^{L}
, which vanishes as
L
tends to infinity. Meanwhile, let the first-order derivative of
\zeta_{L}
be
\dot{\zeta}_{L}
. Because the magnitude of
\dot{\zeta}_{L}
is always
2
, its
L_{2}

norm (with respect to the same Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
) is also
2
.
Thus, the
L_{2}
norm of the derivative function
\dot{\zeta}_{L}
remains a constant, even though the
L_{2}
norm of the function
\zeta_{L}
itself converges exponentially in
L
to zero, yielding the observation in Section
2.3
.

Clearly, this example implies that the
L_{2}
convergence between a neural network function and a target, for instance from
\widehat{g}_{n}
to
m_{0}
, does not necessarily imply the convergence at the same rate between their partial derivatives, for instance from
\widehat{g}_{n,j}
to
m_{0,j}
. Moreover, this discrepancy becomes more apparent as the depth
L
increases, but this is precisely the regime of interest for deep neural networks. Mathematically, this phenomenon can be understood as the absence of a general reverse Poincaré inequality. The inequality itself conventionally bounds the function norm by the norm of the derivative of the said function (see Theorem 13.27 in
Leoni, (2017)
for an exact formulation), but the reverse direction does not always hold, as demonstrated by our simple example.

It’s important to clarify that we’re not asserting that
no
neural network can approximate the first-order derivatives of smooth functions
(
Gühring et al.,, 2020
)
. In our example above, we could easily select a neural network that consistently outputs zero, thereby perfectly approximating both the identically zero function and its derivative. However, in practical scenarios involving observations with noise, there’s no guarantee that such a derivative-approximating neural network will be chosen. Furthermore, as previously noted, for approximating higher-order derivatives, any ReLU-based neural network will completely fail if we simply settle for the derivatives of the same order (which will always be zero almost everywhere) on the network function directly.

Having demonstrated the irregularities that the derivatives of neural networks may exhibit, we now motivate our smoothing operation. Our first motivation is the convergence performance obtained from the smoothing operation and the generality of the operation. As mentioned in Section
1.2
, our smoothing operation proposed in (
10
) is applicable to general machine learning estimators. Specifically, our Lemma
A.4
shows that, if we start from a generic regression estimator
\widehat{g}
for a generic regression function target
m
, then by using the simple smoothing operation in (
10
), we can easily recover a convergence rate from the
derivative
estimator
\widehat{g}_{j}^{\textup{s}}
to the target derivative
m_{j}
through the convergence rate from
\widehat{g}_{n}
to
m_{0}
, up to a factor
1/h
and a bias term; thus the two rates can be made comparable as long as the bandwidth
h
does not approach zero too fast. Therefore, we have translated the power of neural networks for regression function estimation into an analogous result for derivative estimation through smoothing. The bias term occurs naturally because
\widehat{g}_{j}^{\textup{s}}
is the
smoothed
version of the naive derivative estimator
\widehat{g}_{j}
, and hence its target is the
smoothed
target derivative
m_{j}^{\textup{s}}
instead of the non-smoothed target derivative
m_{j}
. Later, we will apply our lemma on our deep neural network estimators, which will replace
\widehat{g}
here with
\widehat{g}_{n}
in (
7
) or
\widecheck{g}_{n}
in (
14
).

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
(i)
and
A.3
hold.
Let
\widehat{g}=\widehat{g}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}):\mathbb{R}^{{\overline{r}}+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a generic estimator that estimates a generic target
m=m({\bm{f}},x_{j}):\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with a
L_{2}
rate of
r_{n}
on an event
\mathcal{A}
, that is

\displaystyle\textstyle\{\int(\widehat{g}-m)^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}\leq r_{n}\penalty\ \text{on the event}\penalty\ \mathcal{A}.

(43)

Moreover, let
m_{j}^{\textup{s}}
and
m_{j}
be respectively the smoothed and non-smoothed derivatives (with respect to the last argument) of the target function
m
, and suppose that
m_{j}^{\textup{s}}
exhibits a bias with respect to
m_{j}
as follows:

\displaystyle\textstyle\{\int_{\Omega_{h}}(m_{j}^{\textup{s}}-m_{j})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}\leq\mbox{Bias}_{n}.

(44)

Then, there exists a constant
c^{\textup{s}}
(that does not depend on
g
or
m
) such that the smoothed derivative estimator
\widehat{g}_{j}^{\textup{s}}
estimates the non-smoothed
m_{j}
with the following rate:

\displaystyle\textstyle\{\int_{\Omega_{h}}(\widehat{g}_{j}^{\textup{s}}-m_{j})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}\leq\frac{c^{\textup{s}}}{h}r_{n}+\mbox{Bias}_{n}\penalty\ \text{on the event}\penalty\ \mathcal{A}.

(45)

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, the left-hand side of (
45
) can be bounded as

\displaystyle\textstyle\{\int_{\Omega_{h}}(\widehat{g}_{j}^{\textup{s}}-m_{j})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}\leq\{\int_{\Omega_{h}}(\widehat{g}_{j}^{\textup{s}}-m_{j}^{\textup{s}})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}+\{\smallint_{\Omega_{h}}(\widehat{m}_{j}^{\textup{s}}-m_{j})^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}.

The first term on the right-hand side above can be bounded by
Lemma
A.1
in conjunction with the rate in (
43
), and the second term on the right-hand side above is simply bounded by the condition given in (
44
).
∎

The second motivation for our smoothing operation comes from traditional non-parametric regression. It is well known that in the latter setting, the optimal bandwidth for the derivative estimator of either the density or regression function should be larger than the optimal bandwidth for the estimator of the density or regression function itself (see, for instance,
(
Gasser and Müller,, 1984
, Theorem 1)
,
(
Hall and Marron,, 1987
, Theorem 3.2)
and
(
Härdle et al.,, 1990
, p. 226)
), due to the former estimator possessing a larger variance at the same bandwidth. Another example in non-parametric regression calling for over-smoothing comes from the so-called irrelevant regressors
(
Li and Racine,, 2006
, Section 2.2.4)
, which are those predictors that do not materially contribute to the regression outcome. Irrelevant regressors are not about derivative estimation per se, but by their very definition they are similar to those coordinates
X_{k}
that satisfy the null hypothesis (
3
) in our screening test. In kernel regression, it is known that the optimal bandwidth for the irrelevant regressors should diverge to infinity in order to “weed out” these regressors more efficiently. (Furthermore, if a regressor is not irrelevant, but contributes linearly to the regression outcome, it’s conjectured that its associated optimal bandwidth should diverge to infinity as well
(
Li and Racine,, 2006
, Section 2.4.1)
.) Thus, from the point of view of both derivative estimation and screening in traditional non-parametric regression, it is not surprising that smoothing could benefit our derivative estimation and screening test.

Appendix B
Supporting details and preliminary proofs for Section
3

B.1
Bias from diversified projection and neural network approximation

In this section we show two parallel results: first, in Lemma
B.1
we show that the true regression function
m_{0}=m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j}):\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is well approximated by
\widetilde{g}_{n}=\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\equiv\widetilde{m}_{n}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
where

\displaystyle\textstyle\widetilde{m}_{n}=\argmin_{m=m({\bm{f}},x_{j})\in\mathcal{F}_{n}(r+1)}\sup_{({\bm{f}},x_{j})\in[-2b,2b]^{r}\times[-b,b]}|(m-m_{0})({\bm{f}},x_{j})|,

(46)

and second, in Lemma
B.4
we show that analogously the true Riesz representer under the null hypothesis
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
is well approximated by

\displaystyle\textstyle\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}=\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\sup_{(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\in[-c_{b}b,c_{b}b]^{\overline{r}}\times[-b,b]}|(\alpha-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})|.

(47)

We start with Lemma
B.1
. Let
\nu_{\textup{a},n}
be the approximation rate in the sup norm attained by
\widetilde{m}_{n}
in (
46
), that is

\displaystyle\textstyle\nu_{\textup{a},n}=\sup_{({\bm{f}},x_{j})\in[-2b,2b]^{r}\times[-b,b]}|(\widetilde{m}_{n}-m_{0})({\bm{f}},x_{j})|.

As can be seen from the lemma and its proof below, the price we pay for the approximation is decomposed into the term
\delta_{\textup{f}}
, which in fact diminishes with respect to the ambient dimension
d
and which results from approximating
m_{0}
using diversified factors through
g_{0}=g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
given in (
8
) (Lemma
B.2
), and the term
\nu_{\textup{a},n}
, which results from subsequently approximating
g_{0}
by the neural network function
\widetilde{g}_{n}
(Lemma
B.3
). Our lemma is a straightforward extension of Ineq. (F.6) in the appendix of
Fan and Gu, (2023)
by including the variable of interest
X_{j}
. For completeness, we will provide a sketch of the proof.

Lemma B.1

(Modification of Inequality (F.6) in
Fan and Gu, (2023)
)
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
to
3.3
and
A.1
to
A.2
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left[\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\delta_{\textup{f}}+\nu_{\textup{a},n}.

Proof.

First, we have the easy bound

\displaystyle\textstyle[\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq[\int\{g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}]^{1/2}+[\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}]^{1/2}.

The first and second term in the last line above are in turn handled by Lemma
B.2
and Lemma
B.3
below respectively, whose rates when combined yield the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Under the same assumptions as Lemma
B.1
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left[\int\{g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\delta_{\textup{f}}.

Proof.

By the definition of diversified projection matrix,
\operatorname{rank}(\mathbf{H}_{j}^{\dagger})=r
,
which implies
\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
.
Starting from the decomposition

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i}=d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{X}}_{i}=d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{B}{\bm{F}}_{i}+d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i}=\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}_{i}+d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i},

(48)

we have

\displaystyle m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle=m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-g_{0}(\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}_{i}+d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle=m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}_{i}+d^{-1}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i},X_{i,j})\quad\mbox{ by the definition of $g_{0}$ in \eqref{eq:def_g0}}

\displaystyle=m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i}+d^{-1}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i},X_{i,j})\quad\mbox{ by the definition of $\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}$}.

(49)

By Assumption
A.1
,
m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})
is Lipschitz in the sense that
|m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{f}}^{\prime},x_{j})|\leq C_{\textup{L}}\|{\bm{f}}-{\bm{f}}^{\prime}\|
(for all
x_{j}
). It follows from this and (
49
) that

\displaystyle|m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})|\leq C_{\textup{L}}d^{-1}\left\|\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq C_{\textup{L}}d^{-1}{\left\|\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\right\|_{\textup{op}}}\left\|\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i}\right\|_{2}.

(50)

By the derivation of (F.15) in
Fan and Gu, (2023)
and Assumption
A.2
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\|_{\textup{op}}\leq\left\{v_{\min}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger})\right\}^{-1}\lesssim 1.

(51)

Next we bound
\|d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i}\|_{2}
in (
50
). It follows from the linearity of expectation and then Assumption
3.3
that

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}_{i}\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\overline{r}}(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\textup{W}_{j,k}u_{i,j})^{2}\right]\textstyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\overline{r}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\textup{W}_{j,k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{i,j}^{2}\right]+\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}\textup{W}_{j,k}\textup{W}_{j^{\prime},k}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{i,j}u_{i,j^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq{\overline{r}}\max_{j,k}\left|\textup{W}_{j,k}\right|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[u_{i,j}^{2}\right]+{\overline{r}}\max_{j,k}\left|\textup{W}_{j,k}\right|^{2}\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[u_{i,j}u_{i,j^{\prime}}\right]\right|\textstyle\lesssim{\overline{r}}d_{u}.

(52)

Finally, we conclude by taking the expectation of the square of both sides of (
50
) and subsequently applying (
51
) and (
52
).
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Under the same assumptions as Lemma
B.1
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left[\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}.

Proof.

Following essentially the same derivation on p. 10 in the appendix of
Fan and Gu, (2023)
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}=\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}}\in[-2b,2b]^{r}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle+\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}}\notin[-2b,2b]^{r}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

By the definition of
\widetilde{g}_{n}
,
g_{0}
and
\nu_{\textup{a},n}
, the first term on the right-hand side above is reduced to

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}}\in[-2b,2b]^{r}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\int\{\widetilde{m}_{n}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}}\in[-2b,2b]^{r}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\leq\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}.

Meanwhile, for the second term, by Assumption
A.1
, Markov’s inequality, and finally the reasoning at the top of p. 11 in the appendix of
Fan and Gu, (2023)
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\{\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{f}}\notin[-2b,2b]^{r}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq(\|\widetilde{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\|_{L_{\infty}}+\|g_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\|_{L_{\infty}})^{2}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{F}}\notin[-2b,2b]^{r})

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim(2M_{\infty})^{2}\mathbb{P}(\|d^{-1}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\|_{2}\geq b)\leq\mathbb{E}[\|d^{-1}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\|_{2}^{2}]\lesssim\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}.

Combining the two equation displays above yields the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Lemma B.4

(Bias for approximating the Riesz representer)
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
to
3.4
and
A.1
to
A.4
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left[\int\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}.

Proof.

As usual, let
\widetilde{F}_{k}
denote the
k
-th element of
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
and moreover let
[\mathbf{W}^{\top}]_{k\cdot}
denote the
k
-th row of
\mathbf{W}^{\top}
. Using the decomposition (
48
),

\displaystyle|\widetilde{F}_{k}|\leq|\mathbf{H}_{k\cdot}{\bm{F}}|+|d^{-1}[\mathbf{W}^{\top}]_{k\cdot}{\bm{u}}|\leq|\mathbf{H}_{k\cdot}{\bm{F}}|+\|d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\|_{2}.

(53)

By Assumption
A.4
, the first term
|\mathbf{H}_{k\cdot}{\bm{F}}|
in the last step above is uniformly bounded over
k\in\{1,\dots,{\overline{r}}\}
by
c_{b}b/2
with probability
1-\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}
. Next, the second term has been bounded in (
52
) and yields, by Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|d^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\|_{2}\geq c_{b}b/2)\lesssim\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}.

Hence, we conclude that with probability at least
1-C\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}
,
\widetilde{\bm{F}}\in[-c_{b}b,c_{b}b]^{\overline{r}}
. Then, using argument similar to that in the proof of Lemma
B.3
, the lemma holds because

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}=\int\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{\bm{f}}\in[-c_{b}b,c_{b}b]^{\overline{r}}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle+\int\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\widetilde{\bm{f}}\notin[-c_{b}b,c_{b}b]^{\overline{r}}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\textstyle\lesssim\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}.\qed

B.2
The stability of conditional expectation in Assumption
A.2

This subsection addresses the issue that while model (
2
) yields the proper centering of the noise, namely
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=0
, after replacing
{\bm{F}}
by
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
we may no longer have
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=0
; instead, what is properly centered here is the adjusted noise term
\widetilde{\epsilon}\equiv Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
. Naturally we will need to handle the difference
|\epsilon-\widetilde{\epsilon}|=|\{Y-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})\}-\{Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]\}|=|\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]|
, whose
L_{2}
norm is precisely the left-hand side of (
37
).
This difference concerns the closeness between the two conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
and
m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
under a perturbation of the conditioning variables.
A simple version of such a “stability of conditional expectation” result appears in
Bryc and Smolenski, (1992)
; however, only one-dimensional random variables were examined and no explicit convergence rate was provided. We will provide in Lemma
B.5
such a stability result which is more appropriate for our context.

For brevity, define
m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
. Compared to the full regression function
m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}}]
defined below (
4
),
m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
is obtained by conditioning on a smoother
\sigma
-algebra (because the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
is contained in the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})
) and in fact
m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})=\mathbb{E}[m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})|{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
. In turn, both
m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})
and
\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
involved in (
37
) can be obtained by further smoothing
m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
(as the proof of Lemma
B.5
will show). Define
{\bm{\Xi}}=\mathbf{W}^{\top}{\bm{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\overline{r}}
. Let
{\bm{F}}
have marginal density
p_{{\bm{F}}}
and let
{\bm{\Xi}}
and
u_{j}
have joint density
p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}
. Further define the random vector
\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}=C_{\textup{f}}^{-1}\delta_{\textup{f}}^{-1}\frac{1}{d}(\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}-\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\bm{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
where
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{\overline{r}}
and
C_{\textup{f}}
is the constant in condition
(v)
in Lemma
B.5
, and the random functions
g_{\textup{n}}
and
g_{\textup{d}}
as, for
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle g_{\textup{n}}(a)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\int m^{*}({\bm{F}}+a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d},\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}\bm{z},X_{j})\,p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{F}}+a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d})p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}){\rm d}\bm{z},

\displaystyle g_{\textup{d}}(a)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\int p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{F}}+a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d})p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}){\rm d}\bm{z}.

The reason we introduce these functions is because, in terms of them, by the derivations in the proof of Lemma
B.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}})}{g_{\textup{d}}(C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}})},\quad\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(0)}{g_{\textup{d}}(0)};

hence, the left-hand side of (
37
) can be expressed with the help of the mean value theorem applied to the expressions above.

Lemma B.5

(Conditions for the validity of inequality (
37
))
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
to
3.3
, and
A.2
excluding (
37
), hold. Moreover, suppose that

(i)

The function
m^{*}=m^{*}({\bm{f}},\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
where
{\bm{f}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\widetilde{\bm{f}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\overline{r}}
is bounded, and
m^{*}
admits a bounded gradient in the first argument
{\bm{f}}
which we denote by
\nabla m^{*}({\bm{f}},\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
;

(ii)

{\bm{F}}
admits a density
p_{{\bm{F}}}
with gradient
\nabla p_{{\bm{F}}}
;

(iii)

{\bm{u}}
and
{\bm{F}}
are independent;

(iv)

{\bm{\Xi}}
and
u_{j}
admits a joint density
p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}=p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}({\bm{\xi}},v_{j})
, and
p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}
admits a partial derivative with respect to
v_{j}
which we denote by
\dot{p}_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}
;

(v)

The following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0<a<C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}}\left\{\frac{\int\nabla p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{F}}+a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d})^{\top}p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d})\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}{\rm d}\bm{z}}{g_{\textup{d}}(a)}\right\}^{2}\right]<\infty,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0<a<C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}}\left\{\frac{\int p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{F}}+a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d})\dot{p}_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d})\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}{\rm d}\bm{z}}{g_{\textup{d}}(a)}\right\}^{2}\right]<\infty;

(vi)

The random vector
{\bm{\Xi}}
satisfies
\|\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}\|_{2}\leq C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}
almost surely for a constant
C_{\textup{f}}
.

Then Inequality (
37
) holds.

Some remarks about the conditions in Lemma
B.5
are in order. First, condition
(vi)
is not directly used in the proof of Lemma
B.5
per se, but it facilitates our subsequent discussion about condition
(v)
. In any case, the proof of Lemma
B.2
shows that
\mathbb{E}\|\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}\|_{2}\lesssim\delta_{\textup{f}}
, so condition
(vi)
is reasonable.
Condition
(v)
may appear complex at first; however, this condition simplifies vastly if we simply set
a=0
. Take the second inequality in condition
(v)
as an example. When
a=0
, the expectation on the left-hand side reduces to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\frac{\int\dot{p}_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j})\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}{\rm d}\bm{z}}{\int p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}){\rm d}\bm{z}}\right\}^{2}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{condition }\ref{exp_stability:con_6}}}{{\lesssim}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\frac{\int\dot{p}_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}){\rm d}\bm{z}}{\int p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}){\rm d}\bm{z}}\right\}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{\frac{\dot{p}_{u_{j}}(u_{j})}{p_{u_{j}}(u_{j})}\right\}^{2}\right]

where
\dot{p}_{u_{j}}
denotes the derivative of the marginal density
p_{u_{j}}
of
u_{j}
and in the last step we have exchanged integration and differentiation. We recognize the rightmost term above as simply the Fisher information for location of the idiosyncratic term
u_{j}
, and the boundedness of this information is a very weak condition. Thus, condition
(v)
can be seen as a slightly strengthened version of the boundededness of this Fisher information under a small perturbation by the random variable
a\mathbf{u}_{{\bm{\Xi}},\bm{z},d}
where
0<a<C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.5
.

By the smoothing property of conditional expectations (see, for instance, property (10) on p. 348 of
Resnick, (2005)
),
\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})
, and similarly
\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})\}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\{\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]\}^{2}].

We now give explicit expressions for
\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
and
\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
. We start from the first of these, namely

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\frac{\int m^{*}({\bm{f}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})p_{{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}({\bm{f}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}){\rm d}{\bm{f}}}{p_{\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})},

(54)

where
p_{\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}
and
p_{{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}
denote respectively the joint densities of
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
and
X_{j}
, and of
{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}}
and
X_{j}
.
Recalling (
48
) which gives
\widetilde{\bm{F}}=\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}{\bm{\Xi}}
, and letting
\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}
denote the
j
-th row of
\mathbf{B}
so
X_{j}=\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{F}}+u_{j}
, we note that for
\widetilde{\bm{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\overline{r}}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
, that
(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})=(\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}{\bm{\Xi}},\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{F}}+u_{j})=(\widetilde{\bm{a}}^{\top},b)^{\top}
is equivalent to
{\bm{\Xi}}=d(\widetilde{\bm{a}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}})
and
u_{j}=b-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{F}}=b-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}-\frac{1}{d}{\bm{\Xi}})
. Then, by the usual convolution argument, the joint density of
p_{\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}
is given by

\displaystyle\textstyle p_{\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}(\widetilde{\bm{a}},b)=\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{a}}{\rm d}b}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}\leq\widetilde{\bm{a}},X_{j}\leq b)=\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{a}}{\rm d}b}\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}{\bm{\Xi}}\leq\widetilde{\bm{a}},\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{F}}+u_{j}\leq b)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{a}}{\rm d}b}\mathbb{P}({\bm{\Xi}}\leq d(\widetilde{\bm{a}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}),u_{j}\leq b-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{F}})=\frac{{\rm d}^{2}}{{\rm d}\widetilde{\bm{a}}{\rm d}b}\int\int^{d(\widetilde{\bm{a}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{f}})}\int^{b-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}}}p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{f}})p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{w},v){\rm d}v{\rm d}\bm{w}{\rm d}{\bm{f}}

\displaystyle\textstyle=d^{\overline{r}}\int p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{f}})p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(d(\widetilde{\bm{a}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{f}}),b-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}}){\rm d}{\bm{f}}

where the last step follows by the Leibniz integral rule. Now,
p_{\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
can serve as the denominator of
\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
in (
54
). Analogously, in the numerator of (
54
), for
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle p_{{\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}}(\bm{a},\widetilde{\bm{a}},b)=d^{\overline{r}}p_{{\bm{F}}}(\bm{a})p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(d(\widetilde{\bm{a}}-\mathbf{H}\bm{a}),b-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}\bm{a}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\int m^{*}({\bm{f}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\,p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{f}})\,p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(d(\widetilde{\bm{F}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{f}}),X_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}}){\rm d}{\bm{f}}}{\int p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{f}})\,p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(d(\widetilde{\bm{F}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{f}}),X_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}{\bm{f}}){\rm d}{\bm{f}}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\left(\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\int m^{*}({\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}-\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\bm{z},\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}\bm{z},X_{j})\,p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}-\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\bm{z})\\
\\
\times p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}(\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}-\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\bm{z})){\rm d}\bm{z}\end{multlined}\right)}{\int p_{{\bm{F}}}({\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}-\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\bm{z})\,p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}-\mathbf{B}_{j\cdot}(\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}{\bm{\Xi}}-\frac{1}{d}\mathbf{H}^{\dagger}\bm{z})){\rm d}\bm{z}}=\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}})}{g_{\textup{d}}(C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}})}.

where in the transition to the second to last step we have conducted a change of the integration variable from
{\bm{f}}
to
\bm{z}=d(\widetilde{\bm{F}}-\mathbf{H}{\bm{f}})
. Meanwhile, for the second conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
, we can analogously obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\dfrac{\int m^{*}({\bm{F}},\mathbf{H}{\bm{F}}+\frac{1}{d}\bm{z},X_{j})\,p_{{\bm{\Xi}},u_{j}}(\bm{z},u_{j}){\rm d}\bm{z}}{p_{u_{j}}(u_{j})}=\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(0)}{g_{\textup{d}}(0)}

Next, denote by
\dot{g}_{\textup{n}}
and
\dot{g}_{\textup{d}}
the derivatives of
g_{\textup{n}}
and
g_{\textup{d}}
respectively. By the mean value theorem, for some random
\widetilde{a}
strictly between
0
and
C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]-\mathbb{E}[m^{*}({\bm{F}},\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]|=\left|\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}})}{g_{\textup{d}}(C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}})}-\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(0)}{g_{\textup{d}}(0)}\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle=\left|\left\{\frac{\dot{g}_{\textup{n}}(\widetilde{a})}{g_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}-\frac{g_{\textup{n}}(\widetilde{a})}{g_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}\frac{\dot{g}_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}{g_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}\right\}\right|C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{condition }\ref{exp_stability:con_1}}}{{\lesssim}}\left\{\left|\frac{\dot{g}_{\textup{n}}(\widetilde{a})}{g_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}\right|+\left|\frac{\dot{g}_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}{g_{\textup{d}}(\widetilde{a})}\right|\right\}C_{\textup{f}}\delta_{\textup{f}}.

Finally, Inequality (
37
) follows by applying condition
(v)
to the last term above.
∎

B.3
Useful bounds on key stochastic terms

We first present a couple of results that will be used repeatedly for bounding some key stochastic terms throughout our proofs, for instance in the proof of Theorem
3.1
in Section
C.1
. Here, the key element is to utilize sharp weighted empirical process techniques in, for instance,
Giné et al., (2003)
to obtain, for each indexing function (for instance
\theta
on the left-hand sides of (
58
) and (
59
)), a bound on the empirical sum of the indexing function that is related to the norm of that particular indexing function (for instance,
\|\theta(Y_{i},{\bm{F}}_{i},{\bm{X}}_{i})\|_{L_{2}}
on the right-hand sides of (
58
) and (
59
)). Bounds of this kind become sharper when the norm of the indexing function itself is small, and are key to obtaining good rates for
\widehat{g}_{n}
and our subsequent derivative estimators.

Define the quantity
\nu_{\textup{s},n}
that will feature prominently in the our subsequent Lemma
B.6
and Proposition
B.7
, where we recall again from Def.
A.3
that
V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
is the VC-index of the subgraphs associated with the neural network class
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
:

\displaystyle\textstyle\nu_{\textup{s},n}=\left\{\frac{V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\log(n)}{n}\right\}^{1/2}.

(55)

Lemma B.6

(Lemma 2 in
Fan and Gu, (2023)
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a class of uniformly bounded functions on
R^{1+r+d}
, for which we can choose a constant (bounded) envelope function
G
. Assume that the class
\mathcal{G}
is of VC-type (Def.
A.4
) with index
v=2V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
(so in particular the covering number for
\mathcal{G}
admits the bound (
36
) with
v=2V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
). Then, there exist some universal constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
c_{3}
such that for all
\sqrt{s^{\prime}}\geq c_{1}\sqrt{n}\nu_{\textup{s},n}/\{\zeta(1-\zeta)\}
with
0<\zeta<1
, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta,s^{\prime}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta,s^{\prime}})\geq 1-c_{2}\exp(-c_{3}\zeta^{2}(1-\zeta)s^{\prime})

(56)

we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall g\in\mathcal{G},\quad\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{g^{2}(Y_{i},{\bm{F}}_{i},{\bm{X}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g^{2}(Y,{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})]\}\right|\leq\zeta\left\{\int g^{2}(Y_{i},{\bm{F}}_{i},{\bm{X}}_{i}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}\right\}.

(57)

Proof.

As hinted in the lemma statement, this is essentially Lemma 2 in
Fan and Gu, (2023)
with minor notational differences. Alternatively, one could rely on Lemma 2 in
Giné et al., (2003)
. We omit the proof.
∎

Recall the adjusted noise
\widetilde{\epsilon}=Y-\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
from Section
B.2
. Then, define
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}=Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}]
,
i=1,\dots,n
, which form i.i.d. copies of
\widetilde{\epsilon}
.

Proposition B.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a class of uniformly bounded functions on
R^{{\overline{r}}+1}
, for which we can choose a constant (bounded) envelope function
G
. Assume that the class
\mathcal{G}
is of VC-type with index
v=2V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
. Then, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{2,s}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{2,s})\geq 1-12e^{-s}
, for a constant
c_{4}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall\theta\in\mathcal{G},\quad\textstyle|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})|\leq c_{4}\left\{\|\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}.

(58)

Moreover, instead let
\mathcal{G}
be a class of uniformly bounded functions on
R^{1+r+d}
, again of VC-type with index
v=2V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
. Then on an event
\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{3,s})\geq 1-12e^{-s}
, for a constant
c_{5}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall\theta\in\mathcal{G},\quad\textstyle\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\theta(Y_{i},{\bm{F}}_{i},{\bm{X}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\theta(Y_{i},{\bm{F}}_{i},{\bm{X}}_{i})]\right\}\right|\leq c_{5}\left\{\|\theta(Y,{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}.

(59)

Proof.

We first prove (
58
). Our main strategy is to apply the proof of Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
. For simplicity we assume that the uniform bound on the magnitudes of functions in
\mathcal{G}
is one, so we could simply choose the constant envelope
G=1
.

We let
s=s_{n}=1
,
l=l_{n}=\lceil\log(\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{-1})\rceil
(where “
\lceil\cdot\rceil
” is the ceiling operator that returns the smallest integer no smaller than the argument),
r=r_{n}=e^{-l_{n}}\in[e^{-1}\nu_{\textup{s},n},\nu_{\textup{s},n}]
, and
\rho_{j}=r_{n}e^{j}
. Define the function classes
\mathcal{G}(a)=\{\theta:\theta\in\mathcal{G},\|\theta\|_{L_{2}}\leq a\}
,
\mathcal{G}(a,b]=\mathcal{G}(b)\setminus\mathcal{G}(a)
, and
\mathcal{G}_{e}(a)=\mathcal{G}(a/e,a]
.

Our general proof strategy based on the aforementioned Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
is to control the stochastic behavior of the empirical process
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
within each “shell”
\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
for the shells numbered
j=1,\dots,l
.

As our first step
, we control the tail probability with respect to the expectation of the (supremum of the) empirical process within each shell. Recall the sub-Gaussian norm
C_{\epsilon}<0
of
\epsilon
from Assumption
A.1
. Also note that the bound
M_{\infty}
on the full conditional expectation
m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})
implies the same bound on any of its smoothed versions including
m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})=\mathbb{E}[m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})|{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
and
\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]=\mathbb{E}[m_{0}^{*}({\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
due to the monotonicity property of conditional expectations. Thus,
\widetilde{\epsilon}-\epsilon=m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})-\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]
is bounded, and so
\widetilde{\epsilon}
is sub-Gaussian as well and we assume its sub-Gaussian norm is a constant
\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}
(with
\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}<\infty
). By first Theorem 4 and then Eq. (13) in
Adamczak, (2008)
, we know that

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|\geq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|+s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{4n\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\|_{L_{2}}^{2}}\right)+3\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{C^{2}\|\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}]}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}|\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})|\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{4n\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}}\right)+3\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{C^{2}\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\|\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\log(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{4n\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}}\right)+3\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{C^{2}\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}^{2}\log(n)}\right).

Then, absorbing
\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}
into
C
and setting
s=\{2\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}\rho_{j}\sqrt{n}+C\log^{1/2}(n)\}\sqrt{u}
yields

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|+\frac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}\rho_{j}\sqrt{u}+C\frac{\log^{1/2}(n)}{n}\sqrt{u}\bigg)\geq 1-4e^{-u}.

(60)

Then, conforming to the notation in the proof of Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
, define the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s)=\Big\{

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|\leq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle+\frac{2\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}}{\sqrt{n}}\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}\rho_{j}+C\frac{\log^{1/2}(n)}{n}\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\Big\}.

Then
\mathbb{P}(\cap_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s))\geq 1-8e^{-s}
.

As our second step
, with the tail probability on hand, we shall bound the expectation
\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|
within each shell.
Further introduce the notation that
\omega_{n}(a)=n^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})|
if
a\in(\rho_{j-1},\rho_{j}]
. We now find a function
\omega:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
that satisfies the conditions in Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
, that is,

(i)

\omega
is non-decreasing;

(ii)

\omega(a)/a
is non-increasing;

(iii)

\omega_{n}(a)\leq\omega(a)
,
\forall a\in[r_{n},s_{n}]
;

(iv)

\sup_{a\in(0,1]}a\sqrt{\log\log(1/a)}/\omega(a)\leq K<\infty
.

We set out to find such a
\omega
.

Note that we can regard
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}/\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon})\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
, (indexed by)
\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
as the empirical process
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
,
\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
symmetrized by the random variables
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}/\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Although the
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}/\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}
’s are not Rademacher random variables, by our earlier argument, when conditioning on the
\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i}
’s and
X_{i,j}
’s, the process
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}/\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon})\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
is nevertheless centered and sub-Gaussian with respect to the (random) distance
d
on
\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
defined as
d^{2}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})=\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}
. Thus, the proof of Theorem 3.5.4 in
Giné and Nickl, (2016)
, in particular the part that invokes Theorem 3.5.1, applies. Specifically, let
Q
be a generic measure on
(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
, and recall that
G=1
is the envelope for the class
\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
(so simply
\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)}=1
, though at times we will retain the dependence on
\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)}
to conform to the notations in
van der Vaart and Wellner, (1996)
and
Giné and Nickl, (2016)
), let
N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau)
be the covering number for the class
\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
by balls of radius
\tau
in the
L_{2}(Q)
norm, and let the constants
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
be as in Theorem 3.5.4 in
Giné and Nickl, (2016)
. We then obtain from that theorem that, if
a\in(\rho_{j-1},\rho_{j}]
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon}}\omega_{n}(a)=\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}/\widetilde{C}_{\epsilon})\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq A_{1}\int_{0}^{\rho_{j}}\sup_{Q}\sqrt{\log\{2N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)})\}}{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\textstyle+A_{2}\left\{\int_{0}^{\rho_{j}}\sup_{Q}\sqrt{\log\{2N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)})\}}{\rm d}\tau\right\}^{2}/\sqrt{n}\rho_{j}^{2}.

By the covering number condition imposed in the proposition, we conclude that for a large enough constant
C_{2}
,

\displaystyle\omega(u)=C_{2}\left\{V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(2/u)u+n^{-1/2}V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\log(2/u)\right\}.

Finally, we put everything together.
Now, on the event
\cap_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s)
,
\forall 1\leq j\leq l
,
\forall\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|\leq 2\omega_{n}({\|\theta\|_{L_{2}}})+2\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}\rho_{j}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+C\frac{\log^{1/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\leq 2\omega(\rho_{j})+(2\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}+C)\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\rho_{j}.

By our condition
(iv)
on
\omega
and the derivations in the proof of Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
, for some positive constant
C_{3}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\max_{1\leq j\leq l}\dfrac{\rho_{j}\sqrt{\log(l-j+1)}}{\omega(\rho_{j})}\leq C_{3}.

Hence, on the same event (that is, the event
\cap_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s)
),
\forall j
,
\forall\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\{2+(2\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}+C)\sqrt{2}C_{3}\}\omega(\rho_{j})+(2\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\|_{L_{2}}+C)\rho_{j}\sqrt{s}

\displaystyle\leq C\left[\left\{V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(n)+\sqrt{s}\right\}\|\theta\|_{L_{2}}+n^{-1/2}V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\log(n)\right].

For the class
\mathcal{G}(r_{n})
, we can just invoke (
60
) but with
\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
replaced by
\mathcal{G}(r_{n})
, to conclude that

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}(r_{n})}|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})|\leq C\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right]\geq 1-4e^{-s}.

Combining the two cases above, we obtain the part of the conclusion of the proposition regarding (
58
). The other part of the proposition regarding (
59
) follows by minor modification. (Here the process on the left-hand side of (
59
) is properly centered for each
\theta
, so we can proceed with the usual symmetrization by Rademacher random variables.)
∎

B.4
The stochastic term in the proof of Theorem
3.2

We recall
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
from (
47
) and that the
(\cdot)_{j}^{s}
as the smoothed
j
-th partial derivative of the argument function as in (
10
).
Define, for
\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
, the function
\theta(\cdot;\alpha):\mathbb{R}^{{\overline{r}}+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
indexed by
\alpha
as

\displaystyle\theta(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j};\alpha)=(\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\mathbbm{1}\{x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}-\{(\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}).

(61)

Note that, under the null hypothesis or not, for each
\alpha
, the function
\theta(\cdot;\alpha)
is always centered under
\mathbb{P}
. Thus, it is not necessary to center
\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\alpha)
at
\mathbb{P}\theta(\cdot;\alpha)=0
in (
63
).

We show the following result that will be useful for bounding the major stochastic term in the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Proposition B.8
.

Assume that Assumptions
3.1
to
3.4
hold, and in addition the bandwidth
h
satisfies

\displaystyle h^{2}V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\log(n)\geq 1.

(62)

Then on an event with probability at least
1-12e^{-s}
, for a constant
c_{6}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),\quad|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\alpha)|\leq c_{6}\left[\left\{\|\theta(\cdot;\alpha)\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}+\frac{1}{h}\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}\right].

(63)

Proof.

We mimic the proof of our Proposition
B.7
. (Alternatively, we could adapt the proof of Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
, which will improve some multiplicative constant.) Define the function class
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\{\theta(\cdot;\alpha):\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\}
. By the form of the function
\theta
, we can take
G=c_{\mathcal{G}}/h
for a constant
c_{\mathcal{G}}
large enough as the envelope for the class
\mathcal{G}_{n}
.

Similar to the proof of Proposition
B.7
but with some minor modifications, we let
s=s_{n}=\|G\|_{L_{2}}=c_{\mathcal{G}}/h
,
l=l_{n}=\lceil\log((c_{\mathcal{G}}/h)\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{-1})\rceil
,
r=r_{n}=s_{n}e^{-l_{n}}\in[e^{-1}\nu_{\textup{s},n},\nu_{\textup{s},n}]
, and
\rho_{j}=r_{n}e^{j}
. Define the function classes
\mathcal{G}(a)=\{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{n},\|\theta\|_{L_{2}}\leq a\}
,
\mathcal{G}(a,b]=\mathcal{G}(b)\setminus\mathcal{G}(a)
, and
\mathcal{G}_{e}(a)=\mathcal{G}(a/e,a]
.

By Theorem 4 in
Adamczak, (2008)
with
\eta=1
(which is in fact allowed),
\delta=1
and
\alpha=2
, we know that

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|n\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|\geq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|n\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|+s\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{4n\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\|\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-\mathbb{E}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\|_{L_{2}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+3\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{C^{2}\|{\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}|\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-\mathbb{E}\theta(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{i,j})|\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{4n\rho_{j}^{2}}\right)+3\exp\left(-\dfrac{s^{2}}{C^{2}c_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}/h^{2}}\right).

Then, for
u>0
, setting
s=\{2\rho_{j}\sqrt{n}+Cc_{\mathcal{G}}/h\}\sqrt{u}
yields

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|\leq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|+\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{n}}\rho_{j}\sqrt{u}+C\dfrac{c_{\mathcal{G}}}{hn}\sqrt{u}\right)\geq 1-4e^{-u}.

(64)

Then, conforming to the notation in the proof of Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
, and essentially by setting
u=s+2\log(l-j+1)
in the event in the probability above, we define the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s)=\Big\{

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|\leq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle+\frac{2\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}}{\sqrt{n}}\rho_{j}+C\frac{c_{\mathcal{G}}}{hn}\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\Big\}.

Then
\mathbb{P}(\cap_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s))\geq 1-8e^{-s}
. Further introduce the notation that
\omega_{n}(a)=n^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-P)\theta|
if
a\in(\rho_{j-1},\rho_{j}]
. We now find a function
\omega:[0,s_{n}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
that satisfies the conditions in Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
, that is,

(i)

\omega
is non-decreasing;

(ii)

\omega(a)/a
is non-increasing;

(iii)

\omega_{n}(a)\leq\omega(a)
,
\forall a\in[r_{n},s_{n}]
;

(iv)

\sup_{a\in(0,1]}a\sqrt{\log\log(1/a)}/\omega(a)\leq K<\infty
.

We set out to find such a
\omega
.

Similar to the proof of Proposition
B.7
, let
N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau)
be the covering number for the class
\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
by balls of radius
\tau
in the
L_{2}(Q)
norm for a generic measure
Q
on
(Y,{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})
, and let the constants
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
be as in Theorem 3.5.4 in
Giné and Nickl, (2016)
. Then, by that theorem, if
a\in(\rho_{j-1},\rho_{j}]
,

\displaystyle\omega_{n}(a)\leq\textstyle A_{1}\dfrac{c_{\mathcal{G}}}{h}\int_{0}^{\rho_{j}/\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)}}\sup_{Q}\sqrt{\log\{2N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)})\}}{\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+A_{2}\dfrac{c_{\mathcal{G}}}{h}\left\{\int_{0}^{\rho_{j}/\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)}}\sup_{Q}\sqrt{\log\{2N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)})\}}{\rm d}\tau\right\}^{2}/\sqrt{n}\rho_{j}^{2}.

By Eq. (
38
),
N(\mathcal{G}_{n},L_{2}(Q),\tau)\leq N(\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),L_{2}(Q),c_{-}h\tau)
for a small enough constant
c_{-}>0
that is not dependent on
Q
. In combination with Theorem 2.6.7 in
van der Vaart and Wellner, (1996)
, we can take, for a large enough constant
C_{1}
and by adjusting
c_{-}
if necessary, for
\mathcal{Q}
the set of all generic measures
Q
on
(Y,{\bm{F}},{\bm{X}})
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\log\{2N(\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j}),L_{2}(Q),\tau\|G\|_{L_{2}(Q)})\}\leq C_{1}V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\log(1/c_{-}h\tau).

(65)

Thus, for a large enough constant
C_{2}
, we can set

\displaystyle\omega(u)=C_{2}\left\{V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}^{1/2}\log^{1/2}\left(\dfrac{1}{h^{2}u}\right)u+\frac{1}{n^{1/2}h}V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\log\left(\dfrac{1}{h^{2}u}\right)\right\}.

Now, on the event
\cap_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s)
,
\forall j
,
\forall\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
, by condition (
62
),

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|\leq 2\omega_{n}(\|\theta\|_{L_{2}})+2\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\rho_{j}+C\dfrac{c_{\mathcal{G}}}{h\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\omega(\rho_{j})+(2+Cc_{\mathcal{G}})\sqrt{s+2\log(l-j+1)}\rho_{j}.

From condition (
62
), we have

1/(h\sqrt{n})\lesssim r_{n}\lesssim\rho_{j}
.
By our condition
(iv)
on
\omega
and the derivations in the proof of Theorem 4 in
Giné et al., (2003)
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\max_{1\leq j\leq l}\dfrac{\rho_{j}\sqrt{\log(l-j+1)}}{\omega(\rho_{j})}\leq C_{3}.

Hence, on the same event (that is, the event
\cap_{j=1}^{l}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{+}(s)
),
\forall j
,
\forall\theta\in\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\{2+(2+Cc_{\mathcal{G}})\sqrt{2}C_{3}\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\omega(\rho_{j})+(2+Cc_{\mathcal{G}})\rho_{j}\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq C\left\{\left(\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right)\|\theta\|_{L_{2}}+\frac{1}{h}\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}\right\}.

In addition, for the class
\mathcal{G}(r_{n})
, we can just invoke (
64
) but with
\mathcal{G}_{e}(\rho_{j})
replaced by
\mathcal{G}(r_{n})
, and simplify with (
62
), to conclude that

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{G}(r_{n})}\sqrt{n}\left|\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta\right|\leq C\left\{\frac{1}{h}\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}\right]\geq 1-4e^{-s}.

Combining the two cases above, we obtain the conclusion of the proposition.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of main theorems and additional results for Section
3

C.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Proof.

We first introduce a shorthand notation that we will employ throughout: for a possibly random function
m=m({\bm{f}},\bm{x})
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}m=\int m({\bm{f}},\bm{x}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}=\int m({\bm{f}},\bm{x}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

Since
\widetilde{\bm{F}}=\widetilde{\bm{F}}({\bm{X}})
, for a possibly random function
g=g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})
, the analogous expression

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}g=\int g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}=\int g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}

makes sense as well. We note that
\mathbb{P}m
and
\mathbb{P}g
could still be random due to the potential inherent randomness of
m
and
g
.

We first prove (
22
)
. Recall
\widetilde{g}_{n}
from Section
B.1
.
To start, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}=\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n})+\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widetilde{g}_{n})+\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widetilde{g}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}+\left\{\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n})\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widetilde{g}_{n}),

(66)

where the inequality follows by the definition of
\widehat{g}_{n}
(in particular that it minimizes the loss
\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;g)
within
g\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
). Next, for the term in the curly bracket in the last step of (
66
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n})=\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}))-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}))-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}^{2}

and, for the last term in the last step of (
66
),

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widetilde{g}_{n})=\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}^{2}.

Plugging the two equalities above into (
66
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}}_{I_{n,1}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}))-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=I_{n,1}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}.

(67)

We next analyze
I_{n,1}
.
Note that the class of functions
\{g-m_{0}=g(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{f}},x_{j}):g\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
B.6
(in particular the covering number bound) by Theorem 2.6.7 in
van der Vaart and Wellner, (1996)
. Thus by Lemma
B.6
, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta,s^{\prime}}
satisfying (
56
), we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall g\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),\quad|\mathbb{P}(g-m_{0})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(g-m_{0})^{2}|\leq\zeta\left\{\mathbb{P}(g-m_{0})^{2}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}\right\}.

(68)

Applying this result with
g=\widehat{g}_{n}
on (
67
), on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}\leq\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+4\underbrace{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})}_{I_{n,2}}+2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}.

(69)

Next, we investigate the rate of the stochastic term
I_{n,2}
on the right-hand side of (
69
). Recalling the
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}
’s introduced in Section
B.3
, we further decompose

\displaystyle\textstyle I_{n,2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\epsilon_{i}-\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i})(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\equiv I_{n,2,1}+I_{n,2,2}.

The reason we need this decomposition is that in
I_{n,2,1}
, the
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}
’s are conditionally properly centered, that is
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}|\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}]=0
, while the term
I_{n,2,2}
will utilize the “stability of conditional expectation” condition in (
37
) to treat the difference
\epsilon_{i}-\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}
.

Now, for the term
I_{n,2,1}
, note that the class of functions
\{(g-\widetilde{g}_{n})=(g-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}):g\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\}
satisfies the covering number bound in Proposition
B.7
for (
58
); we then apply (
58
) in Proposition
B.7
with
\theta=\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}
to conclude that, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{2,s}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{2,s})\geq 1-12e^{-s}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{n,2,1}|=|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})|\leq c_{4}\left\{\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}.

(70)

For the term
I_{n,2,2}
, first note that
|\epsilon_{i}-\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}|=|\{Y_{i}-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}-\{Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}]\}|=|m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]|
, which is uniformly bounded by
2M_{\infty}
by the comments in the proof or Proposition
B.7
. As already mentioned, the class of functions
\{(g-\widetilde{g}_{n})=(g-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}):g\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\}
satisfies the covering number bound in Proposition
B.7
for (
59
); multiply this class by
\epsilon-\widetilde{\epsilon}=\mathbb{E}[Y|\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}]-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
considered as a single, uniformly bounded function changes at most the constant in the covering number bound (see Theorem 3 in
Andrews, (1994)
). Hence, we can apply (
59
) in Proposition
B.7
to conclude that, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{3,s})\geq 1-12e^{-s}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{n,2,2}-\int I_{n,2,2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq c_{4}\left\{\|(\epsilon-\widetilde{\epsilon})(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\left\{\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}

where in the transition to the last line we have again applied the boundedness of
\epsilon-\widetilde{\epsilon}
.
For the centering term
\int I_{n,2,2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}
, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\textstyle|\int I_{n,2,2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}[(\epsilon_{i}-\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i})^{2}]\right\}^{1/2}\left\{\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n})^{2}\right\}^{1/2}\lesssim\delta_{\textup{f}}\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}.

(71)

Collecting the rates for
I_{n,2,1}
and
I_{n,2,2}
, we conclude that on
\mathcal{A}_{2,s}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{n,2}|=|I_{n,2,1}+I_{n,2,2}|\lesssim\left\{\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}+\delta_{\textup{f}}\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}.

Then, applying this result in (
69
), we obtain that on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,s}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
we have (note the change in notation from
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}
to
\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}
below)

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\textstyle\leq\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+C\left\{\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}+C\delta_{\textup{f}}\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}
