\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\|\widehat{g}_{n}-\widetilde{g}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\{\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}+\|\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}\}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\{\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}\}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+2\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}}_{I_{n,3}}

(72)

where in the transition to the last step we have invoked Lemma
B.1
.
For the term
I_{n,3}
, by (
68
) and the remark above it, and then Lemma
B.1
again, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle I_{n,3}=\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}\lesssim\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}.

Then, invoking the above in (
72
) and then using
ab\leq 2^{-1}(a^{2}+b^{2})
several times, we obtain that on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,s}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\textstyle\lesssim\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+\{\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}\}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad\lesssim\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\}+\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}+\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}+\frac{s}{n}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}\}.

Finally, solving for
\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}
yields that, on
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,s}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}\textstyle\lesssim\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}}.

(73)

Next, we impose Assumption
3.2
and derive the concrete resultant rates for
\nu_{\textup{s},n}
in (
55
)
(which then becomes the rate
\nu_{n}
in (
21
)) and
\nu_{\textup{a},n}
. For the former, namely
\nu_{\textup{s},n}
, essentially we need to obtain an upper bound for the VC-index
V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
of the class
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
; see our Def.
A.3
. Note that there are a couple of closely-related though not always identical concepts regarding the VC-index. Our definition turns out to be identical to the
pseudo-dimension
defined in, for instance, Def. 11.2 in
Anthony and Bartlett, (1999)
and Def. 2 in
Bartlett et al., (2019)
for
real
-valued functions. A closely related concept, termed
\textup{VCdim}(\mathcal{G})
for a class
\mathcal{G}
of
\{0,1\}
-valued functions, also appears; for instance, see Theorem 14.1 in
Anthony and Bartlett, (1999)
. Fortunately, we have the following simple connection: let
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\dagger}
be our class of function
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
augmented with one extra input unit and one extra computation unit, and let
\mathcal{H}^{\dagger}
be the resulting set of
\{0,1\}
-valued functions computed by
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\dagger}
, all as described in Theorem 14.1 in
Anthony and Bartlett, (1999)
. Then the VC-index of
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
will be bounded by
\textup{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}^{\dagger})
, that is
V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\leq\textup{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}^{\dagger})
, and the right-hand side is in turn bounded in
Anthony and Bartlett, (1999)
. Specifically, let
U
be the total number of weights for a function in
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
, so following the derivation on p. 2 in appendix B of
Kohler and Langer, (2021)
,
U\sim Lk_{\textup{0}}^{2}
(note that the input dimension
{\overline{r}}+1
does not appear because by assumption
{\overline{r}}
is a constant). Then, by Theorem 7 in
Bartlett et al., (2019)
and then Assumption
3.2
,
V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\leq\textup{VCdim}(\mathcal{H}^{\dagger})\lesssim UL{\log(U)}\sim n^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\log^{2\frac{4\kappa-1}{2\kappa+1}+1}(n)
. To fix the multiplicative factor in this bound on
V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}
, we shall explicitly set

\displaystyle V_{\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)}\leq c_{\textup{p}}n^{\frac{1}{2\kappa+1}}\log^{2\frac{4\kappa-1}{2\kappa+1}+1}(n)=p_{n}\log^{-1}(n)

(74)

for a large enough constant
c_{\textup{p}}
and for
p_{n}
from (
21
).

Now we are ready to fix
s
and
s^{\prime}
in (
73
). We set
s=p_{n}
so
\sqrt{s/n}
precisely becomes the concrete rate
\nu_{n}
in (
21
) (up to a factor involving
c_{\textup{p}}
), and
\nu_{n}
in turn bounds
\nu_{\textup{s},n}
from (
55
) if we apply in (
55
) the bound (
74
). Similarly, we choose
s^{\prime}
to be a large enough multiple of
p_{n}
. Together with (
73
), we then conclude that, on the intersection
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,s}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
whose probability is at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}\|_{L_{2}}\textstyle\lesssim\nu_{n}+\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}.

(75)

Finally, we derive in (
75
) the rate of
\nu_{\textup{a},n}
in order to reach (
22
). This rate is implicitly implied in Corollary 1 in
Fan and Gu, (2023)
, and depends on the earlier Proposition 3.3 in
Fan et al., (2022)
, but we give a brief derivation here. Define
\widetilde{L}
and
\widetilde{k}_{\textup{0}}
such that
\widetilde{L}\log(\widetilde{L})=L
and
\widetilde{k}_{\textup{0}}\log(\widetilde{k}_{\textup{0}})=k_{\textup{0}}
; we introduce these quantities because the neural network architecture scaling in Proposition 3.3 in
Fan et al., (2022)
is specified by such quantities. Under Assumption
3.2
, it’s easy to compute that
\widetilde{L}\widetilde{k}_{\textup{0}}\sim n^{\frac{1}{4\kappa+2}}\log^{\frac{4\kappa-1}{2\kappa+1}-2}(n)
,
which further yields, by Proposition 3.3 in
Fan et al., (2022)
,

\displaystyle\nu_{\textup{a},n}\lesssim(\widetilde{L}\widetilde{k}_{\textup{0}})^{-2\kappa}\sim\nu_{n}.

(76)

Finally, applying the bound above in (
75
) yields that (
22
) holds on the intersection
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,s}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,s}
.

Finally, given (
22
), a straightforward application of Lemma
A.4
with
\widehat{g}=\widehat{g}_{n}
and
m=m_{0}
will yield (
23
). This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Proof.

Introduce the shorthand notation
\mathbb{P}_{n|\mathcal{I}_{h}}
for the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
but only including the samples with sample indices in
\mathcal{I}_{h}
. Then, the loss function
\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\cdot)
introduced in Section
2.4
can be written as

\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha)=\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha^{2}-2\mathbb{P}_{n|\mathcal{I}_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}.

Also recall
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
defined in (
47
). Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}=\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n})+\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n})-\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})+\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n})}_{I_{n,2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}}_{I_{n,3}}+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2},

(77)

where the inequality is due to
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
minimizing the loss
\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\cdot)
.
Now, for handling the terms
I_{n,2}
and
I_{n,3}
in the above, note that for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}(\alpha-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha^{2}-2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\alpha\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})+\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha_{n}^{2\textup{null}}+2\mathbb{P}_{n|\mathcal{I}_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}-\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\alpha\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})+\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha_{n}^{2\textup{null}}+2\mathbb{P}_{n|\mathcal{I}_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}.

Then, together using the function
\theta(\cdot;\alpha)
introduced in (
61
), the sum of the terms
I_{n,2}
and
I_{n,3}
becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n})+\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})+2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})+2\mathbb{P}_{n|\mathcal{I}_{h}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}-2\mathbb{P}_{n|\mathcal{I}_{h}}\widetilde{\alpha}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}=2\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n}).

(78)

Plugging (
78
) into (
77
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}}_{I_{n,1}}+2\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n})+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}.

(79)

For the term
I_{n,1}
above, first note that the class of functions
\{\alpha-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}:\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
B.6
. Then, similar to (
68
), by Lemma
B.6
, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta,s^{\prime}}
satisfying (
56
), we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),\quad|\mathbb{P}(\alpha-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}-\mathbb{P}_{n}(\alpha-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}|\leq\zeta\left\{\mathbb{P}(\alpha-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}\right\}.

(80)

Applying this result with
\alpha=\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
for
I_{n,1}
on (
79
), on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+2\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n})+\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

or equivalently, rearranging and centering we have,

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}\leq\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+4\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n})+2\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}+4\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{n}\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n})}_{I_{n,4}}+2\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}}_{I_{n,5}}.

(81)

Next, we consider the term
I_{n,4}
in (
81
). First, we note that

\displaystyle\|\theta(\cdot;\alpha)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{(\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\mathbbm{1}\{X_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}-(\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[(\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{X_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}+\{(\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n})\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\right]\lesssim h^{-2}\|\alpha-\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

where the last inequality follows from (
38
). Then, applying Proposition
B.8
(whose condition (
62
) is satisfied due to Assumptions
3.2
,
3.5
and the paragraph below (
73
)) and the result above on the term
(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n})
in (
81
), we obtain that, on an event
\mathcal{A}_{2,s}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{2,s})\geq 1-12e^{-s}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{n,4}|

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq c_{4}\left\{\|\theta(\cdot;\widehat{\alpha}_{n})\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}\lesssim\left\{\frac{1}{h}\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}+\frac{1}{h}\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}.

Next, we consider the term
I_{n,5}
in (
81
), which represents a population bias induced by approximating
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
through the neural network function
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}
. By (
80
) and the remarks above, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{n,5}|=|\mathbb{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}|\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}\lesssim\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}

where the last step follows by Lemma
B.4
. Then, collecting the rates for
I_{n,4}
and
I_{n,5}
in (
81
), we obtain, on the intersection
\mathcal{A}_{1,\zeta=\frac{1}{2},s^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,s}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim h^{-1}(\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}})\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}+\frac{1}{h}\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}+\nu_{\textup{a},n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2}+\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}.

Then, solving for
\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}
yields that

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim h^{-1}(\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}})+\nu_{\textup{a},n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}+\sqrt{\frac{s^{\prime}}{n}}.

This is our counterpart to (
73
) in the current proof of Theorem
3.2
. Then, we apply Assumption
3.2
as in the paragraph below (
73
), and subsequently choose
s
and
s^{\prime}
, and bound
\nu_{\textup{s},n}
, as in the paragraph below (
74
), and bound
\nu_{\textup{a},n}
as in (
76
). This will yield the rate, holding on an event with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, on the left-hand side of (
27
) except for the cap at
M
. The additional cap is straightforward due the boundedness assumption on
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
3.2
regarding (
27
).

Next, we bound
\|\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\|_{L_{2}}
which will subsequently lead to (
29
). Recall the population loss
R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha)=\mathbb{E}\alpha^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-2\int_{\Omega_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}
(i.e., the first two terms in the last line of (
11
)) introduced in the proof of Lemma
A.3
. We have, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})^{2}=\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})^{2}-R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})+R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})-R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha_{t,n}^{*})+R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})^{2}-R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})+R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle=\|\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}-2\langle\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle+\|\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}-\|\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+2\langle\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}},\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\rangle+\|\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}-2\langle\alpha_{t,n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\langle\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle+2\langle\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\rangle

\displaystyle\textstyle=-2\int_{\Omega_{h}}e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}+2\int_{\Omega_{h}}(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle=-2\int_{\Omega_{h}}[e^{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})}-1](\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 2\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}\|(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*})_{j}^{\textup{s}}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\frac{2c^{\textup{s}}}{h}\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}\|\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\|_{L_{2}},

(82)

where the inequality is due to
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
minimizing the loss
R_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\cdot)
(see the discussion in the proof of Lemma
A.3
), the transition to the third to last line follows by the property of
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
as the Riesz representer in (
25
) and the corresponding property of
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
, the transition to the last line follows by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and the last step follows by Lemma
A.1
. Then, combining (
82
) with the boundedness assumption on
\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
and
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
yields (
28
).

Finally, applying the triangle inequality on (
27
) and (
28
) yields the remaining conclusion, namely (
29
), of Theorem
3.2
.
∎

C.3
Proof of Proposition
3.3

Proof.

We first prove the first half of the proposition on
\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}
.
By definition,

\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}=\argmin_{\delta_{\textup{t}}\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n}+\delta_{\textup{t}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}).

Denote the function
\widehat{\delta}_{n}=\widehat{g}_{n}-m_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n}+\delta_{\textup{t}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n})=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{Y_{i}-\widehat{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-\delta_{\textup{t}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{Y_{i}-\widehat{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right\}^{2}-2\delta_{\textup{t}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{Y_{i}-\widehat{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right\}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+\delta_{\textup{t}}^{2}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}).

The last line above viewed as a quadratic function in
\delta_{\textup{t}}
reaches its minimum at
\delta_{\textup{t}}=\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}
for
\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}
given in (
15
). We can also re-write (
15
) as

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\epsilon_{i}-\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right\}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

(83)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\underbrace{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})}_{J_{n,1}}}\underbrace{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})}_{J_{n,2}}-\dfrac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})}\underbrace{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})}_{J_{n,3}}.

For the term
J_{n,1}
(in the denominator) above, similar to (
80
) (with the choice
s^{\prime}
being a large enough multiple of
p_{n}
), we conclude that on an event
\mathcal{A}_{1,1}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{1,1})\geq 1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\geq\frac{1}{2}\int\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}-C\nu_{n}^{2}.

Next,

\displaystyle\textstyle\int\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}\geq\int(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}+\int(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}-(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2})(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\int(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}+\int(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}+\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle\geq\int(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}-CM_{\infty}\{\int(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}^{1/2}\geq\int(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}-C(h^{-1}\nu_{n}+\delta_{\textup{f}})

where the last step holds on an event
\mathcal{A}_{1,2}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{1,2})\geq 1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
by Theorem
3.2
.
Combining the above two inequalities, by the conditions imposed in Proposition
3.3

with Assumption
3.6
(i)
, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{1,2}
we have that
J_{n,1}
is bounded from below from zero by a constant (independent of
n
).

Next, for the term
J_{n,2}
, we can follow the treatment for the terms
I_{n,2}=I_{n,2,1}+\{I_{n,2,2}-\int I_{n,2,2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}+\int I_{n,2,2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
in Section
C.1
, together with the choice of
s
in that proof, to conclude that on an event
\mathcal{A}_{2}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\geq 1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle|J_{n,2}|\lesssim\{\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\}\nu_{n}+\{\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\}\nu_{n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}\|\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim r_{m,n}

where the last step follows by the boundedness of
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
.

For the term
J_{n,3}
above, first note that the class of functions
\{(g-m_{0})\alpha:g,\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
B.7
for (
59
) (with minor adjustment of constants) by Theorem 3 in
Andrews, (1994)
and our assumptions. Then, by (
59
) in Proposition
B.7
, together with the choice of
s
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
in Section
C.1
, we conclude that an event
\mathcal{A}_{3}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{3})\geq 1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|J_{n,3}-\int J_{n,3}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|\lesssim\{\|\widehat{\delta}_{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\}\nu_{n}\lesssim\{M\|\widehat{\delta}_{n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\}\nu_{n}\lesssim r_{m,n},

\displaystyle\textstyle|\int J_{n,3}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|\lesssim\{(\mathbb{P}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2})(\mathbb{P}\widehat{\delta}_{n}^{2})\}^{1/2}\lesssim Mr_{m,n}\lesssim r_{m,n}

both hold. Combining these results, we conclude that
|\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}|\lesssim r_{m,n}
on an event with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
.

Next, we prove the second half of the proposition on the minimization condition (
16
).

Denote the functions
\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}=\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}
,
\delta_{\alpha,n,t}=\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
and
\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}=\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{t,n}^{*}=\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}+\delta_{\alpha,n,t}
. It follows from the definition of
\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}
that
\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n})\leq 0
.
We then have, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n})}+\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n}+\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n}+\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n}+(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\widehat{\alpha}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widehat{g}_{n}+(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\mp{r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}})\equiv J_{n,t},

where the inequality follows by our earlier remark.

Next,

\displaystyle\textstyle J_{n,t}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\{\epsilon_{i}-\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle-\{\epsilon_{i}-\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\textstyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\mp 2\left\{\epsilon_{i}-\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right\}r_{\textup{inf},n}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-r_{\textup{inf},n}^{2}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\right]

\displaystyle\textstyle=\pm(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\frac{2r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\mp\frac{2r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad\pm\frac{2r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-\frac{r_{\textup{inf},n}^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\textstyle=\pm(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})\frac{2r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\mp\frac{2r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad\pm\frac{2r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+(\pm 2\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}+r_{\textup{inf},n})\frac{r_{\textup{inf},n}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\equiv\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}(\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n})J_{n,t,1}\mp 2r_{\textup{inf},n}J_{n,t,2}+2r_{\textup{inf},n}J_{n,t,3}+r_{\textup{inf},n}(\pm 2\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}+r_{\textup{inf},n})J_{n,t,4}

where we have introduced

\displaystyle\textstyle J_{n,t,1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}),\quad J_{n,t,2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}),

\displaystyle\textstyle J_{n,t,3}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}),\quad J_{n,t,4}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n,t}^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}).

We only demonstrate the treatment for
J_{n,t,1}
in detail; the treatments for the other
J_{n,k}
’s are in principle similar and have been employed in the proofs of Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
and Proposition
3.3
. For the term
J_{n,t,1}
, we further define
J_{n,t,1,1}=\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}
and
J_{n,t,1,2}=\mathbb{P}_{n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\delta_{\alpha,n,t}
so
J_{n,t,1}=J_{n,t,1,1}+J_{n,t,1,2}
. Now, we apply (
59
) in Proposition
B.7
to conclude that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},|J_{n,t,1,1}|=|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}+\mathbb{P}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}|\lesssim\{\|\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\}\nu_{n}+\{\mathbb{P}\alpha_{t,n}^{*2}\mathbb{P}\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}^{2}\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\|\widehat{\delta}_{\alpha,n}\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}.

To treat
J_{n,t,1,2}
, we apply the same proposition to conclude that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},\quad|J_{n,t,1,2}|=|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\delta_{\alpha,n,t}+\mathbb{P}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\delta_{\alpha,n,t}|\lesssim\left\{\|\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\delta_{\alpha,n,t}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\right\}\nu_{n}+\{\mathbb{P}\alpha_{t,n}^{*2}\mathbb{P}\delta_{\alpha,n,t}^{2}\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\nu_{n}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}}.

Thus, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},\quad|J_{n,t,1}|\lesssim r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}}.

For the other
J_{n,t,k}
terms, we can similarly conclude that, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},\quad|J_{n,t,2}|\lesssim r_{m,n}(r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}}),\quad|J_{n,t,3}|\lesssim r_{m,n}(r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}}),\quad|J_{n,t,4}|\lesssim r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}(r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}\nu_{n}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}}).

Combining all the
J_{n,t,k}
terms and further simplifying, we conclude that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},\quad|J_{n,t}|\lesssim r_{\textup{inf},n}r_{m,n}(r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}}).

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

C.4
Proof of Theorem
3.4

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, given (
30
), the proof of (
31
) is straightforward, so we will only prove (
30
). By Proposition
3.3
and Assumption
3.6
, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{\textup{t},1}
(see Proposition
3.3
) we have
|\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}|\leq 1
, and hence further by (
14
), on the same event,

\displaystyle\widecheck{g}_{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)\equiv\{g+\delta_{\textup{t}}\alpha:g,\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),|\delta_{\textup{t}}|\leq 1\}.

We now control the complexity of the class
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
.
Let
Q
be a generic measure on
(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
, let
\mathcal{N}
be a cover of
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
by balls of radius
\tau/\sqrt{9}
in the
L_{2}(Q)
norm and moreover
\mathcal{N}
consist of functions uniformly bounded in magnitude by
M
, and let
\mathcal{E}
be a cover of the interval
[-1,1]
by intervals of length at most
\tau/(\sqrt{9}M)
; we choose
\mathcal{E}
to have cardinality at most
\lceil 2\sqrt{9}M/\tau\rceil
.

Suppose
g,g^{\prime},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
be functions of
(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
, that are uniformly bounded in magnitude by
M
, and moreover satisfy
\|g-g^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}\leq\tau/\sqrt{9}
and
\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}\leq\tau/\sqrt{9}
, and let
\delta_{\textup{t}},\delta_{\textup{t}}^{\prime}\in[-1,1]
be such that
|\delta_{\textup{t}}-\delta_{\textup{t}}^{\prime}|\leq\tau/(\sqrt{9}M)
. Let
\theta=g+\delta_{\textup{t}}\alpha
and
\theta^{\prime}=g^{\prime}+\delta_{\textup{t}}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}\leq 3\|g-g^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}+3\delta_{\textup{t}}^{2}\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}+3(\delta_{\textup{t}}-\delta_{\textup{t}}^{\prime})^{2}\|\alpha^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 3\|g-g^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}+3\delta_{\textup{t}}^{2}\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{L_{2}(Q)}^{2}+3(\delta_{\textup{t}}-\delta_{\textup{t}}^{\prime})^{2}M^{2}\leq 3\frac{\tau^{2}}{9}+3\frac{\tau^{2}}{9}+3\frac{\tau^{2}}{9}=\tau^{2}.

Thus
N(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),L_{2}(Q),\tau)\leq N(\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),L_{2}(Q),\tau/\sqrt{9})^{2}\lceil 2\sqrt{9}M/\tau\rceil
where we follow the notation for the covering numbers, for instance
N(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),L_{2}(Q),\tau)
, in (
36
). Thus, the logarithm of the covering number
N(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),L_{2}(Q),\tau)
for the class
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
is bounded up to a multiplicative constant by the logarithm of the covering number for the class
\mathcal{F}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
. Then, the proof of (
30
) in Theorem
3.4
follows that of (
22
) in Theorem
3.1
with minor modifications.
∎

C.5
Proof of the size under the null: Theorem
3.5

Let
r=r(\cdot;b)
be an unspecified, possibly random function that may change for each occurrence, but that always satisfies
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}}|r(t;b)|\lesssim b
.
By this convention, the function
r=r(t;{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1))
in (
84
) satisfies
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}}|r(t;{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1))|={\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

We will first prove the following master approximate i.i.d. expansion for the statistic
\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})
: under the null hypothesis
H_{0}
in (
3
), with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, uniformly over all
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\sqrt{n}\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})=\frac{t}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+r(t;{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1)).

(84)

Recall the notations
\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}
from (
17
),
\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}
from (
24
), and the random variable
Z_{t,j,h}
from (
26
). Introduce, for a generic function
m:\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the functional
\eta_{t}^{\textup{s}}
as

\displaystyle\textstyle\eta_{t}^{\textup{s}}(m)=\int_{\Omega_{h}}[\exp\{t\,m_{j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-1]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

Under either
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
, we have the master decomposition

\displaystyle\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})=\left\{\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})\right\}+\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})\right\}+\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})-\eta_{t}^{\textup{s}}(m_{0})\right\}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+\{\eta_{t}^{\textup{s}}(m_{0})-\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left[\exp\{t\,m_{0,j}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-1\right]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\}+\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left[\exp\{t\,m_{0,j}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-1\right]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\equiv I_{t,n,1}+I_{t,n,2}+\underbrace{I_{t,n,3}+I_{t,n,4}+I_{t,n,5}}_{=\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{under $H_{0}$}}}{{=}}0}

(85)

where we note that
\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})=\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}
(by a remark in the proof of Lemma
A.3
,
\Psi
in (
24
) can be discarded if
g=g_{0}
). In this decomposition,

i)

I_{t,n,1}
is a centered empirical process evaluated at the random function
\widecheck{g}_{n}
. Under the null,
I_{t,n,1}
admits a fast rate converging to zero because here the smoothed derivative estimator
\widecheck{g}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}
is expected to be close to zero. Under the alternative,
I_{t,n,1}
still converges to zero due to the averaging effect, though the rate will be comparatively slower.

ii)

I_{t,n,2}
is the dominant term in the decomposition under the null, and in fact will admit an approximate i.i.d. expansion that will in turn become the leading, i.i.d. sum in (
84
).

iii)

The term
I_{t,n,3}
represents the approximation error arising from replacing the latent factor
{\bm{F}}
by the diversified factor
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
,
I_{t,n,4}
is a bias term arising from smoothing the true regression function derivative
m_{0,j}
, and finally,
I_{t,n,5}
can be regarded as the original signal strength favoring the alternative. Together, the (magnitude of the) sum
I_{t,n,3}+I_{t,n,4}+I_{t,n,5}
can be regarded as containing the signal favoring the alternative. Here, under the null, the sum becomes
\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})=\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}
which is zero (see the remark below (
26
)).

Next, the treatments of the terms
I_{t,n,1}
and
I_{t,n,2}
will be covered in Sections
C.5.1
and
C.5.2
respectively. The transition from (
32
) to (
33
) will be covered in Section
C.5.3
by showing that the estimated variance
\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}
is a consistent estimator of the unknown asymptotic variance
\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}
. We finalize our proof of Theorem
3.5
afterward.

C.5.1  Proof for the first term in (
85
)

Now we handle the term
I_{t,n,1}
in the decomposition (
85
), which as stated below (
85
) is a centered empirical process term. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}=[-T,-\delta]\cup[\delta,T]
where
T
is a constant.

Proposition C.1
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
3.5
hold. Then, under either
H_{0}
or
H_{1}
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, for a constant
c_{4,1}
, and uniformly over
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{t,n,1}|\leq c_{4,1}\{\frac{|t|}{h}r_{m,n}+\frac{1}{h}\nu_{n}+\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}\}\nu_{n}.

Proof.

Define the function
\theta(\cdot;g,t)=\theta(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j};g,t)=\{\exp[t\,\Psi\{g_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}]-1\}\mathbbm{1}\{x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}:\mathbb{R}^{{\overline{r}}+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
(essentially the summand in (
17
)) indexed by
g\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1)
and
t
. Then,
I_{t,n,1}=(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\theta(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n},t)
. Now, in the proof of Theorem
3.4
in Section
C.4
, it was shown that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,
\widecheck{g}_{n}
belongs to the functional class
\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}(\bar{r}+1)
defined in that proof. We shall focus on this event.

As in the proof of Theorem
3.4
, let
g,g^{\prime}
be functions of
(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})
, and moreover let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
. Then, by Assumption
A.5
,

\displaystyle|\theta(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j};g,t)-\theta(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j};g^{\prime},t^{\prime})|=|\exp[t\,\Psi\{g_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}]-\exp[t^{\prime}\,\Psi\{g^{{}^{\prime}\textup{s}}_{j}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}]|\mathbbm{1}\{x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}

\displaystyle\lesssim t|(g-g^{\prime})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})|\mathbbm{1}\{x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}+|t-t^{\prime}|\leq T|(g-g^{\prime})_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})|\mathbbm{1}\{x_{j}\in\mathcal{B}_{h}\}+|t-t^{\prime}|.

Since
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
is compact, the class of functions
\{\theta(\cdot;g,t):g\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\}
admits a uniform covering number similar to that in (
65
). Thus, the situation is largely similar to that in Proposition
B.8
, and we obtain that on an event with probability at least
1-12e^{-s}
, for a constant
c_{6}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall g\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n}({\overline{r}}+1),\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},\quad|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\theta(\cdot;g,t)|\leq c_{6}^{\prime}[\left\{\|\theta\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{\textup{s},n}\right\}\left\{\nu_{\textup{s},n}+\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}\right\}+\frac{1}{h^{2}}\nu_{\textup{s},n}^{2}].

Choosing
s
as in the proof in Section
C.1
of Theorem
3.1
in the above, we obtain that, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, by changing the constant
c_{6}^{\prime}
if necessary,

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta},\quad|I_{t,n,1}|\leq c_{6}^{\prime}\{\|\theta(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n},t)\|_{L_{2}}+\frac{1}{h}\nu_{n}\}\nu_{n}.

(86)

Now, with the aid of (
38
) and the definition of
Z_{t,j,h}
from (
26
),

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\theta(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n},t)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\int_{\Omega_{h}}\{\exp(t\,\Psi\{\widecheck{g}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\})-1\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq 2\int_{\Omega_{h}}\{\exp(t\,\Psi\{\widecheck{g}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\})-\exp(t\,\Psi\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}+2\int_{\Omega_{h}}\{\exp(t\,\Psi\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},{\rm d}x_{j})\})-1\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim t^{2}\int_{\Omega_{h}}\{(\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0})_{j}^{\textup{s}}\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}+\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{t^{2}}{h^{2}}\int(\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0})^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}+\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim\frac{t^{2}}{h^{2}}\int\{(\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0})^{2}+(g_{0}-m_{0})^{2}\}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}+\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{t^{2}}{h^{2}}r_{m,n}^{2}+\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}^{2},

where the last step follows by Theorem
3.4
and Lemma
B.2
.
Combining the above with (
86
) then yield the conclusion of the proposition.
∎

C.5.2  Proof for the second term in (
85
)

Recall that
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}=\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j}):\mathbb{R}^{{\overline{r}}+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the Riesz representer that satisfies (
25
).

Proposition C.2
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
3.5
hold. Then, under either
H_{0}
or
H_{1}
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, for a common function
r=r(\cdot;b)
as introduced at the beginning of Section
C.5
, uniformly over
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle I_{t,n,2}=t\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+|t|\,r(t;r_{m,n}r_{\alpha,t,n}+\delta_{\textup{f}})+(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2}).

(87)

Proof.

To streamline our presentation, we divide the proof into two parts. In the first part, Lemma
C.3
, the proposition is proven under a set of technical conditions, while in the second part, Lemma
C.4
, the technical conditions themselves are verified. The general direction of the proof of Lemma
C.3
follows that of
Chen et al., (2014)
;
Chen et al., 2022b ()
, but here we work with the adjusted regression function estimator
\widecheck{g}_{n}
instead of the preliminary estimator
\widehat{g}_{n}
, and also take into account the accuracy loss of using
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
in place of
{\bm{F}}
. More importantly, by using the adjusted estimator
\widecheck{g}_{n}
, we avoid artificially imposing the “undersmoothing/bias vanishing abnormally fast” condition as in
Chen et al., 2022b ()
; more specifically, see how their “undersmoothing” condition, Assumption 4.4, is invoked in the proof of their Lemma C.1(ii). If we were to impose a similar condition, then we would need
\nu_{\textup{a},n}=\nu_{\textup{a},n,\textup{undersmooth}}
from (
76
), the bound on the bias, to be
{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(n^{-1/2})
; this would lead to a scaling of
\widetilde{L}\widetilde{k}_{\textup{0}}
that would further result in
\nu_{\textup{s},n}=\nu_{\textup{s},n,\textup{undersmooth}}
from (
55
), the accompanying standard deviation for the stochastic variation, to be at least on the order of
n^{-\frac{2\kappa-1}{4\kappa}}
(up to log factors). Clearly, the rates for
\nu_{\textup{a},n,\textup{undersmooth}}
and
\nu_{\textup{s},n,\textup{undersmooth}}
in the bias/variance decomposition are no longer balanced, and in particular the rate for
\nu_{\textup{s},n,\textup{undersmooth}}
is inferior to our balanced rate
\nu_{n}
in (
21
); in fact, when
\kappa<1/2
, it is no longer true that
\nu_{\textup{s},n,\textup{undersmooth}}={\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
, so the regression function estimators
\widehat{g}_{n}
and
\widecheck{g}_{n}
are no longer consistent.

Lemma C.3
.

Suppose that Assumption
3.6
(ii)
holds, and that on an event
\mathcal{A}
, uniformly over
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq r(t;r_{\textup{inf},n}r_{m,n}r_{\alpha,t,n})

(88)

(note that (
88
) is essentially the minimization condition (
16
) at
t
for the tolerance
b_{n}
on the order of
r_{m,n}(r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}+r_{\alpha,t,\textup{b}})
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})\}\hskip 85.35826pt

\displaystyle\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle{\mp 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\mathbb{E}[\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})}]

\displaystyle=r(t;r_{\textup{inf},n}^{2}),

(89)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\left\{\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})\mp 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle=r(t;r_{\textup{inf},n}r_{m,n}\nu_{n}),

(90)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-{\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}-\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}]

\displaystyle=(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2}),

(91)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}]

\displaystyle=t\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle.

(92)

Then, on the intersection of the event
\mathcal{A}
and another event with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, uniformly over
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
, (
87
) in Proposition
C.2
hold.

Proof.

By condition (
90
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})=\mathbb{P}\{\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})\}\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\{\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\}

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\left\{\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})\mp 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\{\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})\}\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\{\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\}+r(t;r_{\textup{inf},n}r_{m,n}\nu_{n}).

Combining the above with condition (
89
) and Assumption
3.6
(ii)
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\mathbb{P}_{n}\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})=\mp 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\mathbb{P}_{n}\{\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\}+r(t;r_{\textup{inf},n}r_{m,n}\nu_{n}).

By the above and the minimization condition (
88
), we further obtain

\displaystyle\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle-\mathbb{P}_{n}\{\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\}=r(t;r_{m,n}r_{\alpha,t,n}).

(93)

Next, we show that
t\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle
can approximate
\eta_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-\eta_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})
well. First, by conditions (
91
) and (
92
),

\displaystyle I_{t,n,2}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})=\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}]+\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})-\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}]\right\}

\displaystyle=t\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle+(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2}).

Combining the above with (
93
), and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality followed by Lemma
B.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle I_{t,n,2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=t\langle\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle+(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2})

\displaystyle\textstyle=t\,r(t;r_{m,n}r_{\alpha,t,n})+(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2})+t\langle m_{0}-g_{0},\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\rangle+t\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\textstyle=t\,r(t;r_{m,n}r_{\alpha,t,n}+\delta_{\textup{f}})+(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2})+t\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j}).

Compared to (
87
), we only need to replace the
\epsilon_{i}
’s by the
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}
’s in the last line above. This can be done similarly to how we dealt with the term
I_{n,2,2}
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
in Section
C.1
, and is why the intersection of
\mathcal{A}
with another event with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
in the statement of Lemma
C.3
is needed, at the cost of another
t\,r(t;\delta_{\textup{f}})
term. Eventually we arrive at

\displaystyle\textstyle I_{t,n,2}-t\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})

\displaystyle\textstyle=t\,r(t;r_{m,n}r_{\alpha,t,n}+\delta_{\textup{f}})+(|t|+t^{2})r(t;h^{-2}r_{m,n}^{2}),

which is the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
3.5
hold. Then, conditions (
88
), (
89
), (
90
), (
91
) and (
92
) hold with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
.

Proof.

As stated in Lemma
C.3
,
Ineq. (
88
) is essentially the minimization condition (
16
), which has already been established in Proposition
3.3
. Next, we verify condition (
89
). We have

\displaystyle\ell(Y,\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j};\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(Y,\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j};\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*})=\{Y-\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\}^{2}-\{Y-\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\mp r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\}^{2}

\displaystyle=[\epsilon-\{\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})\}]^{2}-[\epsilon-\{\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})\}\mp r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})]^{2}

\displaystyle=\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}[\epsilon-\{\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})\}]\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-r_{\textup{inf},n}^{2}\alpha_{t,n}^{*2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})

\displaystyle=\mp 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\{\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})\}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})-r_{\textup{inf},n}^{2}\alpha_{t,n}^{*2}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j})\pm 2r_{\textup{inf},n}\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}(\widetilde{\bm{F}},X_{j}).

(94)

Then, (
89
) follows by taking the integral of (
94
) against the measure
\mathbb{P}
.

Next, we verify condition (
90
). Starting from Eq. (
94
),

\displaystyle(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\left\{\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n})-\ell(\cdot;\widecheck{g}_{n}\pm r_{\textup{inf},n}\alpha_{t,n}^{*}){\mp}2r_{\textup{inf},n}\epsilon\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle={\mp}2r_{\textup{inf},n}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\{(\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0})\alpha_{t,n}^{*}\}-r_{\textup{inf},n}^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\alpha_{t,n}^{*2}=r(t;r_{\textup{inf},n}r_{m,n}\nu_{n}),

where the last step holds with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, and follows by (at this point) the usual application of (
59
) in Proposition
B.7
, with
r_{m,n}
from Theorem
3.4
.

Next, we verify condition (
91
). In condition (
91
), the second-order remainder term is, for some
\tau^{\prime}
strictly between
0
and
1
, and
v=\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\tau^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{h}}[e^{t\,\Psi\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}-1]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]\Big|_{\tau=\tau^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\displaystyle\left[\int_{\Omega_{h}}t\dot{\Psi}\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}v_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})e^{t\,\Psi\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\right]\right]\Big|_{\tau=\tau^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\int_{\Omega_{h}}\Big[t\ddot{\Psi}\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}(v_{j}^{\textup{s}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})e^{t\,\Psi\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+t^{2}\dot{\Psi}^{2}\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}(v_{j}^{\textup{s}})^{2}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})e^{t\,\Psi\{(g_{0}+\tau v)_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}}\Big]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\bigg]\Big|_{\tau=\tau^{\prime}},

which by Assumption
A.5
and the boundedness of
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
is bounded in magnitude and up to a multiplicative factor (that is not dependent on
t
) by
(|t|+t^{2})\int_{\Omega_{h}}v_{j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}
. Thus, condition (
91
) is verified because, by the first half of (
38
) in Lemma
A.1
applied to this result,

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})-\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})-\frac{\partial\widetilde{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(g_{0})}{\partial g}[\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}]\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim(|t|+t^{2})\|(\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0})_{j}^{\textup{s}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\lesssim(|t|+t^{2})\frac{1}{h^{2}}\|\widecheck{g}_{n}-g_{0}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\lesssim(|t|+t^{2})\frac{1}{h^{2}}r_{m,n}^{2}.

Finally, we verify condition (
92
).
We just need to recall the first part of Lemma
A.3
, from which condition (
92
) follows as a special case. This completes the proof of Lemma
C.4
.
∎

Then, as mentioned earlier, combining Lemmata
C.3
and
C.4
completes the proof of Proposition
C.2
.
∎

C.5.3  Variance estimation

Proposition C.5
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
3.5
hold. Then, under either
H_{0}
or
H_{1}
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}-\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}={\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1).

Hence, under
H_{0}
, we are free to replace
\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}
in (
32
) by
\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}
to arrive at (
33
).

Proof.

We start from the overall decomposition

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}-\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})+\epsilon_{i}-\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})^{2}-\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\underbrace{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})^{2}}_{J_{n,1}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad-2\underbrace{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}\{\widecheck{g}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})-m_{0}({\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})^{2}}_{J_{n,2}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\quad+\underbrace{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\epsilon_{i}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})^{2}-\mathbb{P}\{\epsilon^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}\}]}_{J_{n,3}}+\underbrace{\textstyle\mathbb{P}\{\epsilon^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}\}-\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}}_{J_{n,4}}

\displaystyle=J_{n,1}-2J_{n,2}+J_{n,3}+J_{n,4}.

The term
J_{n,1}
can be treated similarly to how we dealt with the term
I_{n,1}
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
in Section
C.1
, specifically by invoking Lemma
B.6
and the choice of
s^{\prime}
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. We conclude that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle|J_{n,1}|

\displaystyle\leq|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})[\{\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0}\}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}]|+\mathbb{P}[\{\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0}\}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{P}[\{\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0}\}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}]+\nu_{n}^{2}+\mathbb{P}[\{\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0}\}^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}]\lesssim r_{m,n}^{2}M^{2}\lesssim r_{m,n}^{2}.

The term
J_{n,2}
can be treated similarly to how we dealt with the term
I_{n,2}
in the proof of Theorem
3.1
. We conclude that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,

\displaystyle|J_{n,2}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\{\|(\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}\|_{L_{2}}+\nu_{n}\right\}\nu_{n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}\|(\widecheck{g}_{n}-m_{0})\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim r_{m,n}^{2}.

The term
J_{n,3}
is a centered empirical process
(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\{\epsilon^{2}\alpha^{2}\}
evaluated at the random function
\alpha=\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
. This time neither Lemma
B.6
nor Proposition
B.7
directly applies because the factor
\epsilon^{2}
involved is sub-exponential instead of sub-Gaussian. Nevertheless one can still apply Theorem 1 in
Adamczak, (2008)
to conclude that with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,
|J_{n,3}|\lesssim\nu_{n}
.

Finally, for the term
J_{n,4}
, first using the sub-Gaussianity of
\epsilon
and then the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{P}\{\epsilon^{2}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}\}-\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}|\lesssim|\mathbb{P}\{\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}-(\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}\}|=|\mathbb{P}\{(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}+\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})\}|

\displaystyle\lesssim[\mathbb{P}\{(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}+\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}\}\mathbb{P}\{(\widehat{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}})^{2}\}]^{1/2}\lesssim r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}

where the last step holds with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
by Theorem
3.2
.

Collecting terms, we conclude that on an event with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
,
|\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}^{2}-\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}|\lesssim r_{m,n}^{2}+\nu_{n}+r_{\alpha,n}^{\textup{null}}
which is easily
{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
under condition
(

∗

)
‣
3.5
in Theorem
3.5
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Now we are ready to put everything together to complete the proof of the component of Theorem
3.5
regarding the fixed-
t
test (
18
). Under the null, as argued earlier in the proof of Theorem
3.5
, the sum
I_{t,n,3}+I_{t,n,4}+I_{t,n,5}=0
in (
85
). Next, (under the null) the leading term in (
87
) in Proposition
C.2
becomes
t\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})
, while the term
I_{t,n,1}
and the remainder terms in (
87
) are bounded by
{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(n^{-1/2})
with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
by Propositions
C.1
and
C.2
and condition
(

∗

)
‣
3.5
of Theorem
3.5
. This concludes the i.i.d. representation in (
84
). Next, (
32
) follows from (
84
) by an application of a central limit theorem followed by a straightforward replacement of
\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}
by
\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}
based on (
37
). Finally, the transition from (
32
) to (
33
) is covered by Proposition
C.5
in Section
C.5.3
.

Finally, we prove the component of Theorem
3.5
regarding the square test involving
\widehat{\chi}^{2}
in (
20
); the proof for the sup test involving
\widehat{Z}
in (
19
) is analogous.
By the earlier (
84
), with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, uniformly over all
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{n}{t^{2}}\{\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})\}^{2}=\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}^{2}+\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{i}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i},X_{i,j})\}r(t;{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1))+r(t;{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(1))^{2}.

Therefore, by the central limit theorem and the Slutsky’s theorem,

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\mathcal{T}_{\delta}}\frac{n}{t^{2}}\{\widecheck{\eta}_{t}^{\textup{s}}(\widecheck{g}_{n})\}^{2}w(t){\rm d}t\textstyle\rightarrow_{d}\left\{\int_{\mathcal{T}_{\delta}}w(t){\rm d}t\right\}\|\widetilde{\epsilon}\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\chi_{1}^{2}

which, after replacing
\widetilde{\epsilon}
by
\epsilon
as in the proof of the fixed-
t
case, is equivalent to the last equation in (
32
). Again by Proposition
C.5
, we are further free to replace
\|\epsilon\alpha_{n}^{\textup{null}}\|_{L_{2}}
in (
32
) by
\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}
.

∎

C.6
Power of the fixed-
t
test under the alternative

In this subsection, our principal Theorem
C.6

guarantees that the power of our fixed-
t
test in (
18
) approaches one under all local alternatives satisfying conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
in the theorem that place mild conditions on how fast
|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
, the signal strength favoring the alternative, can decay. Similar results for the ensemble tests (
19
) and (
20
) can be shown using analogous techniques and we omit the details here.

Theorem C.6
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
3.5
hold. Furthermore, suppose that at some
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
, for large enough constants
C_{\textup{alt},1}
and
C_{\textup{alt},2}
,

(i)

C_{\textup{alt},1}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\leq|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
;

(ii)

C_{\textup{alt},2}\{\delta_{\textup{f}}+\frac{1}{h^{2}}(\nu_{n}^{2}+\delta_{\textup{f}}^{2})+\frac{1}{h}(\nu_{n}+\delta_{\textup{f}})\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}\}\leq|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
.

Then, under the alternative hypothesis
H_{1}
in (
4
), with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}-\frac{1}{n}
our fixed-
t
test in (
18
) will reject; thus the power of the test tends to one.

Condition
(i)
in the theorem is satisfied as long as
|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
is a large enough multiple of
\sqrt{\log(n)/n}
. In condition
(ii)
, the signal strength
Z_{t,j,h}
appears on both sides; however,
\|Z_{t,j,h}\|_{L_{2}}
on the left-hand side, which admittedly is no smaller than
|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
on the right-hand side, is scaled by
\nu_{n}+\delta_{\textup{f}}
which we could expect to converge to zero. Thus, condition
(ii)
is mild as well. It is worth noting that condition
(i)
is itself implied by, for large enough constants
C_{\textup{alt},1}^{\prime}
and
C_{\textup{alt},3}
,

(a)

C_{\textup{alt},1}^{\prime}\sqrt{\log(n)/n}\leq|\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left[\exp\{t\,m_{0,j}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-1\right]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|
;

(b)

C_{\textup{alt},3}\{\frac{1}{h}\delta_{\textup{f}}+r_{\textup{b},m,j}\}\leq|\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left[\exp\{t\,m_{0,j}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-1\right]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|
for
r_{\textup{b},m,j}
introduced above Theorem
3.1
.

Here, condition
(b)
states that the effect of diversification, represented by
\delta_{\textup{f}}
, and the effect of smoothing the true derivative, represented by
r_{\textup{b},m,j}
, should be weak compared to the original signal. We also prove the remarks above on conditions
(a)
and
(b)
following the proof of Theorem
C.6
.

Proof of Theorem
C.6
.

The original decomposition (
85
) in the proof of Theorem
3.5
remains, but under the alternative the treatment of the various constituent terms could be different.
Now,
|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
is the signal strength favoring the alternative; thus, in contrast,
I_{t,n,1}+I_{t,n,2}
becomes the “noise” under the alternative. The sum of the left-hand sides of conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
in the statement of Theorem
C.6
is, up to a multiplicative factor and with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}
, a bound on the magnitude of the noise
I_{t,n,1}+I_{t,n,2}
. The bound can be straightforwardly established by Propositions
C.1
and
C.2
which hold under the alternative as well. We only remark that

i)

The necessity of condition
(i)
is due to the fact that the leading i.i.d. term within
I_{t,n,2}
now also becomes a part of the noise that could be bounded through Hoeffding’s inequality by the left-hand side of condition
(i)
with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n}
;

ii)

The left-hand side of condition
(ii)
is similar to the left-hand side in condition
(

∗

)
‣
3.5
in Theorem
3.5
and is a bound on the term
I_{t,n,1}
and the remainder terms in (
87
), but note that here, naturally, instead of requiring the bound to be on the order of
{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}}}(n^{-1/2})
under the null, under the alternative we require the bound to become insignificant as compared to the signal
|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
, which is exactly what condition
(ii)
requires.

Then, if the signal
|I_{t,n,3}+I_{t,n,4}+I_{t,n,5}|=|\mathbb{E}Z_{t,j,h}|
is strong enough, as required by conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
, with probability at least
1-Ce^{-p_{n}}-\frac{1}{n}
our test in (
18
) will reject, leading to the conclusion of Theorem
C.6
.

Next, we establish how condition
(i)
is implied by conditions
(a)
and
(b)
. To establish condition
(i)
, it suffices that
|I_{t,n,5}|=|\int_{\Omega_{h}}\left[\exp\{t\,m_{0,j}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-1\right]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}|
is strong enough, which is implied by condition
(a)
, and moreover
|I_{t,n,5}|
dominates
I_{t,n,3}
and
I_{t,n,4}
. We first consider the term
I_{t,n,3}
, which arises due to the diversified projection. By a remark in the proof of Lemma
A.3
,
\Psi
in (
24
) can be discarded if
g=g_{0}
, so

\displaystyle I_{t,n,3}\textstyle=\int_{\Omega_{h}}[\exp\{t\,g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})\}-\exp\{t\,m_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}]{\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

Then, by Taylor expansion and the boundedness of
m_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}
and
g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}
derived in the same remark, followed by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Lemma
A.1
and then Lemma
B.2
,

\displaystyle\textstyle|I_{t,n,3}|\leq Ct\int_{\Omega_{h}}|g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{f}},x_{j})|{\rm d}\mathbb{P}\leq Ct[\int_{\Omega_{h}}\{g_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}(\widetilde{\bm{f}},x_{j})-m_{0,j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{f}},x_{j})\}^{2}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}]^{1/2}\leq C\frac{t}{h}\delta_{\textup{f}},

where the constant
C
is not dependent on
t
.

We next consider the term
I_{t,n,4}
. By arguments similar to those used in establishing the bound for
I_{t,n,3}
above, but instead using the quantity
r_{\textup{b},m,j}
introduced above Theorem
3.1
following the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{t,n,4}\leq Ctr_{\textup{b},m,j}

(where the constant
C
again is not dependent on
t
).

Therefore, under condition
(b)
,
|I_{t,n,5}|
indeed dominates
I_{t,n,3}
and
I_{t,n,4}
. This concludes the proof of the remarks following Theorem
C.6
.
∎

Appendix D
Low-dimensional regime as a special case

D.1
Conditional screening in low-dimension

Our low-dimensional regime refers to the scenario where the dimension
d
is small. This regime can be handled as a special case of our high-dimensional regime through straightforward modifications. First, (when
d
is small) in our regression model (
2
) we can simply set
{\bm{F}}={\bm{X}}_{-j}
where
{\bm{X}}_{-j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
denotes the vector
{\bm{X}}
but without the
j
-th coordinate. Then, since we observe all coordinates of
{\bm{X}}
, the “latent”
{\bm{F}}
is in fact observed. Consequently, in model (
2
),
m_{0}({\bm{F}},X_{j})=m_{0}({\bm{X}})=\mathbb{E}[Y|{\bm{X}}]
, and
r=d-1
. Then, if we wish to screen the
j
-th coordinate
X_{j}
, our conditional screening hypotheses in (
3
) and (
4
) become accordingly

\displaystyle H_{0}:\text{for all}\penalty\ \bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\penalty\ m_{0,j}(\bm{x})=0\penalty\ \text{identically},\penalty\ \text{against}

\displaystyle H_{A}:\text{for some}\penalty\ \bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\penalty\ \text{we have}\penalty\ m_{0,j}(\bm{x})\neq 0.

We summarize the practical implementation of our conditional screening test in the low-dimensional regime in Algorithm
4)
. Also assume that, in place of Assumption
3.1
, we have
{\bm{X}}\in[-b,b]^{d}
. Then the corresponding theoretical development can be obtained a special case of our theoretical results for the high-dimensional regime, by setting
\widetilde{\bm{F}}_{i}
to
{\bm{X}}_{i,-j}
, the preliminary regression and adjusted regression estimators
\widehat{g}_{n}
and
\widecheck{g}_{n}
to
\widehat{m}_{n}
and
\widecheck{m}_{n}
in Algorithm
4)
respectively, and
\delta_{\textup{f}}=0
. For brevity, we omit presenting the theoretical results in the low-dimensional regime.

Input:

Observed samples
Y_{i},{\bm{X}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
; testing if the
j
-th coordinate
{\bm{X}}_{j}
has additional contributions.

Result:

Preliminary and refined regression estimators
\widehat{m}_{n}(\cdot)
and
\widecheck{m}_{n}(\cdot)
, their smoothed derivative estimators
\widehat{m}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}(\cdot)
and
\widecheck{m}_{n,j}^{\textup{s}}(\cdot)
,
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}(\cdot)
, and test results.

1)

Estimate the regression function
m_{0}
in (
2
) by the neural network estimator
\widehat{m}_{n}
defined as

\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{m}_{n}=\argmin_{m\in\mathcal{F}_{n}(d)}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{Y_{i}-m({\bm{X}}_{i})\right\}^{2}.

(95)

Conduct cross validation (see Remark
A.4
), or otherwise, to find a smoothing bandwidth
h
.

2)

Define the loss function
\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\cdot)
as

\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha^{2}({\bm{X}}_{i})-2\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{X}}_{i}),

which can be approximated via Lemma
A.2
, and let the estimator
\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
of
\alpha_{t,n}^{*}
be

\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{\alpha}_{n}=\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{n}(d)}\widehat{R}_{n}^{\textup{null}}(\alpha).

3)

Return
\widecheck{m}_{n}=\widehat{m}_{n}+\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}
, where

\displaystyle\textstyle\widehat{\delta}_{\textup{t}}=\dfrac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}^{2}({\bm{X}}_{i})}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{Y_{i}-\widehat{m}_{n}({\bm{X}}_{i})\right\}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}({\bm{X}}_{i}),

4)

Let
\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}=[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\widehat{\epsilon}_{i}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}({\bm{X}}_{i})\}^{2}]^{1/2}
be the estimated standard deviation. Then, for a prescribed significance level
0<\alpha<1
, for the fixed
t
test, reject the null hypothesis (
3
) in our conditional screening hypotheses if

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{n^{1/2}}{t\|\widehat{\epsilon}\widehat{\alpha}_{n}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n})}}|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=\mathcal{I}_{n}}[\exp\{t\,\Psi(\widecheck{m}_{j}^{\textup{s}}({\bm{X}}_{i}))\}-1]|>z_{1-\alpha/2},

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of a standard normal distribution.
The decision rules for the ensemble tests are analogous to those in (
19
) and (
20
) for the high-dimensional regime, and are omitted.

Algorithm D.1

Partial derivative estimation & conditional screening test by deep neural network in the low dimensional regime
